<SYSTEM>This is the abridged developer documentation for FieldStruct and Actionable</SYSTEM>

# Gliders<span class="af-hero-sub">FieldStruct and Actionable</span>

> A family of Ruby gems that is just getting started. FieldStruct makes typed plain-Ruby objects, Actionable builds typed service objects on top of them, and adapters, in preview, carry both into JSON Schema, Avro, Protobuf, OpenTelemetry and LLM tools.

A glider has no engine. It stays up by using the air that is already there, and these gems try to work the same way: with your data and your system, not against them. That is [the Gliders idea](/explanation/gliders/). ## FieldStruct: typed POROs [Section titled “FieldStruct: typed POROs”](#fieldstruct-typed-poros) Declare typed fields on a plain Ruby class and you get an object that coerces what it is given and knows whether it is valid. There is no database in the picture, and no Rails. hut.rb 
```ruby
class Hut < FieldStruct::Base
  required :name, :string
  optional :places, :integer
end


hut = Hut.new(name: 'Larch Col', places: '24')
hut.places                          # => 24
hut.valid?                          # => true


unnamed = Hut.new(name: '  ')
unnamed.errors.full_messages        # => ["Name is required"]
```
 The places count came in as a string and is an integer now. A blank name didn’t raise - it was recorded, and the message is waiting when you ask for it. ## Actionable: service objects [Section titled “Actionable: service objects”](#actionable-service-objects) An action is a handful of plain methods, one per step, and every run ends in exactly one result: success, failure or skip. The input is a FieldStruct, so a step never sees bad input. reserve_places.rb 
```ruby
FREE_PLACES = { 'KR12' => 6 }


class ReservePlaces < Actionable::Action
  input do
    required :hut_code, :string
    required :places, :integer
  end


  fails :fully_booked, 'not enough free places'


  step :check_room
  step :confirm


  private


  def check_room
    fail!(:fully_booked) if input.places > FREE_PLACES.fetch(input.hut_code, 0)
  end


  def confirm
    succeed("Reserved #{input.places} at #{input.hut_code}")
  end
end


ReservePlaces.run(hut_code: 'KR12', places: '4').message   # => "Reserved 4 at KR12"
ReservePlaces.run(hut_code: 'KR12', places: 40).code       # => :fully_booked
```
 `fail!` records the failure and stops, so `confirm` doesn’t run for the party of forty. The failure code is plain data, which makes it easy to branch on. ## Install [Section titled “Install”](#install) Both gems go in your Gemfile. They are pre-releases for now, so the constraint names the pre-release version. 
```ruby
gem 'field_struct', '~> 1.0.0.pre1'
gem 'actionable-rb', '~> 2.0.0.pre1'
```
 The second gem is `actionable-rb` because another project owns the short name, but you still `require 'actionable'`. Both need Ruby 3.3 or newer. This site documents field_struct 1.0.0.pre1 and actionable-rb 2.0.0.pre1, and CI executes every runnable example on it, the two above included, against those versions. A wrong `# =>` comment fails the build. ## Adapters [Section titled “Adapters”](#adapters) A set of optional gems reads the same field metadata and writes other formats: JSON Schema, Avro, OpenTelemetry, Protobuf, plus actions exposed as LLM and MCP tools. None is released yet. Start at the [adapters overview](/adapters/), or jump to [building your own adapter](/adapters/build-your-own/).

# A typed Hut

> Declare a Hut with typed fields, watch values coerce from strings, and see what happens when they can't.

We start with the smallest piece of the hut network: one hut. By the end of this chapter we’ll have a `Hut` that takes string input from a form, turns it into real Ruby values, and tells us what is wrong when it can’t. ## Where we left off [Section titled “Where we left off”](#where-we-left-off) Nothing yet. This is the first chapter and it needs only `field_struct`, so open `irb` with `require 'field_struct'` or put the code in a script. ## Declare the fields [Section titled “Declare the fields”](#declare-the-fields) A struct is a class that inherits from `FieldStruct::Base`. Every field has a name and a type, and it is either `required` or `optional`. hut.rb 
```ruby
class Hut < FieldStruct::Base
  required :name, :string
  optional :places, :integer
end


hut = Hut.new(name: 'Larch Col', places: 24)
hut.name     # => "Larch Col"
hut.valid?   # => true
```
 `required` means the field has to be present for the hut to be valid. It doesn’t stop you from building a hut without it, as we’ll see in a moment. ## Coerce strings [Section titled “Coerce strings”](#coerce-strings) Form and query params arrive as strings, and assigning a string to an `:integer` field converts it. 
```ruby
form = { name: 'Larch Col', places: '24' }
Hut.new(**form).places   # => 24
```
 The conversion runs on every assignment, in `new` and in the setters, so `hut.places = '30'` leaves `30` behind. ## Add a code, a distance and a season [Section titled “Add a code, a distance and a season”](#add-a-code-a-distance-and-a-season) A distance in kilometres is a measurement, so we use `:decimal`. A float would hand us the `0.1 + 0.2` problem. Four options earn their place here: * `format:` checks a string against a pattern * `round:` rounds a number to that many decimal places * `in:` limits a number to a range * `default:` fills in a value when none is given The season is a `:symbol` limited to a list with `enum:`. hut.rb 
```ruby
class Hut < FieldStruct::Base
  required :name, :string
  required :code, :string, format: /\A[A-Z]{2}\d{2}\z/
  required :distance_km, :decimal, round: 1
  optional :places, :integer, in: 1..400
  optional :season, :symbol, enum: %i[summer winter year_round]
  optional :altitude, :integer, in: 0.., default: 0
end
```
 Caution A bare `0..` at the end of a line makes Ruby read the next line as the end of the range. Here a comma follows it, so we’re safe. When an endless range is the last thing on its line, wrap it in parentheses: `in: (0..)`. Now a hut built from the strings a form would send. 
```ruby
hut = Hut.new(
  name: 'Larch Col',
  code: 'KR12',
  distance_km: '7.25',
  places: '24',
  season: 'summer'
)


hut.distance_km      # => BigDecimal('7.3')
hut.places           # => 24
hut.season           # => :summer
hut.altitude         # => 0
hut.valid?           # => true
```
 The distance `7.25` rounded to `7.3`, `'summer'` became the symbol `:summer`, and `altitude` took its default. ## Read the errors [Section titled “Read the errors”](#read-the-errors) Now we feed it bad input. 
```ruby
bad = Hut.new(name: '  ', code: 'k1', distance_km: '3.5', places: 9_000, season: 'monsoon')


bad.valid?                      # => false
bad.errors.full_messages.size   # => 4
```
 `errors.full_messages` gives one sentence per problem: the blank name, the malformed code, the place count outside `1..400` and the season missing from the list. Nothing raised. Building a hut is never the same as validating it. For one field at a time, use `errors.to_h`. 
```ruby
bad.errors.to_h[:name]    # => ["is required"]
bad.errors.to_h[:places]  # => ["is invalid"]
bad.errors.to_h.keys      # => [:name, :code, :places, :season]
```
 A blank string counts as missing, which is why the name reads “is required” and not “is invalid”. ## When a value can’t be coerced [Section titled “When a value can’t be coerced”](#when-a-value-cant-be-coerced) What if `places` is `'plenty'`? There is no integer to make out of that. 
```ruby
odd = Hut.new(name: 'Larch Col', code: 'KR12', distance_km: '3.5', places: 'plenty')


odd.places                      # => "plenty"
odd.valid?                      # => false
odd.errors.to_h[:places].size   # => 1
```
 By default the raw value stays in the field and the error is recorded. The object remains usable, and your code decides whether to show the error, ask again or give up. The messages never echo what was typed. If you’d rather have an exception, a class can ask for it with `coercion_policy :raise`. We keep the default for the hut network. ## What you built [Section titled “What you built”](#what-you-built) A `Hut` with required and optional fields, four types (`:string`, `:decimal`, `:integer`, `:symbol`) with the options `format:`, `round:`, `in:`, `enum:` and `default:`, string coercion, and an errors object that reports problems without raising. Next we’ll put huts into bookings and send them as JSON. ## See also [Section titled “See also”](#see-also) * [Bookings and JSON](/tutorial/bookings-and-json/): the next chapter * [Fields and options](/reference/field-struct/fields-and-options/) * [Types](/reference/field-struct/types/) * [Coercion versus validation](/explanation/coercion-and-validation/)

# Bookings and JSON

> Nest structs inside a booking, send it as JSON with camelCase keys, and keep an access token out of the logs.

Hikers don’t book one night at a time, they book a route, and a route is a list of legs. In this chapter we nest structs, see how one invalid leg makes its booking invalid, send a booking to another service as JSON, and keep a secret out of the logs. ## Where we left off [Section titled “Where we left off”](#where-we-left-off) We had a `Hut` with a name, a code, a decimal distance and an altitude. Pages don’t share state, so we declare it again here, a little shorter. hut.rb 
```ruby
class Hut < FieldStruct::Base
  required :name, :string
  required :code, :string, format: /\A[A-Z]{2}\d{2}\z/
  required :distance_km, :decimal, round: 1
  optional :altitude, :integer, in: 0.., default: 0
end
```
 ## A leg [Section titled “A leg”](#a-leg) A leg says which hut and for how many nights. Its type is another struct, so we pass the class where a type name would go. booking.rb 
```ruby
class Leg < FieldStruct::Base
  required :hut_code, :string, format: /\A[A-Z]{2}\d{2}\z/
  required :nights, :integer, in: 1..7
end


class Booking < FieldStruct::Base
  required :contact, :email
  required :legs, :array, of: Leg
end
```
 An array field takes `of:`, which can be a type name or a struct class. Plain hashes go in and the nested structs come out. 
```ruby
booking = Booking.new(
  contact: 'lead@example.com',
  legs: [{ hut_code: 'KR12', nights: '2' }, { hut_code: 'EG07', nights: 1 }]
)


booking.legs.size              # => 2
booking.legs.first.nights      # => 2
booking.legs.first.class       # => Leg
booking.valid?                 # => true
```
 ## A bad leg makes a bad booking [Section titled “A bad leg makes a bad booking”](#a-bad-leg-makes-a-bad-booking) Let’s push the second leg out of range, to 30 nights. 
```ruby
bad = Booking.new(
  contact: 'lead@example.com',
  legs: [{ hut_code: 'KR12', nights: 2 }, { hut_code: 'EG07', nights: 30 }]
)


bad.valid?                          # => false
bad.errors.to_h                     # => {legs: ["is invalid"]}
bad.legs.last.errors.to_h           # => {nights: ["is invalid"]}
bad.legs.first.valid?               # => true
```
 The booking only says that `legs` is invalid. The details live on the child that is wrong, so ask the legs when you need to know which one. ## JSON out and back in [Section titled “JSON out and back in”](#json-out-and-back-in) `to_json` writes the booking as JSON and `from_json` reads it back through `new`, so every coercion and validation rule still applies. 
```ruby
json = booking.to_json
json   # => '{"contact":"lead@example.com","legs":[{"hut_code":"KR12","nights":2},{"hut_code":"EG07","nights":1}]}'


copy = Booking.from_json(json)
copy == booking               # => true
copy.legs.last.nights         # => 1
```
 Equal structs have equal values all the way down, which makes the round trip easy to test. ## camelCase for another service [Section titled “camelCase for another service”](#camelcase-for-another-service) The partner desk we meet in chapter 6 wants `contactEmail` and `legList`. We keep snake_case in Ruby and map the names for JSON only. `serialize :json` comes after the fields it names. 
```ruby
class DeskRequest < FieldStruct::Base
  required :contact_email, :email
  required :leg_list, :array, of: Leg
  serialize :json, contact_email: 'contactEmail', leg_list: 'legList'
end


request = DeskRequest.new(contact_email: booking.contact, leg_list: booking.legs.map(&:to_h))
request.as_json.keys                 # => [:contactEmail, :legList]
request.contact_email                # => "lead@example.com"
DeskRequest.from_json(request.to_json) == request   # => true
```
 ## Keep a token out of the logs [Section titled “Keep a token out of the logs”](#keep-a-token-out-of-the-logs) The desk wants an access token. Mark it `sensitive: true` and `inspect`, `pp` and loggers print `[FILTERED]` in its place. 
```ruby
class DeskClient < FieldStruct::Base
  required :base_url, :url
  required :access_token, :string, sensitive: true
end


client = DeskClient.new(base_url: 'https://desk.example.org', access_token: 'tok_ridge_5521')
client.inspect       # => '#<DeskClient base_url: "https://desk.example.org", access_token: [FILTERED]>'
client.access_token  # => "tok_ridge_5521"
```
 The reader still returns the real value because your code needs it. The mask is for display only, and `client.to_json` writes the real token too, so don’t log that. ## What you built [Section titled “What you built”](#what-you-built) A `Booking` that nests `Leg` structs, one invalid child making its parent invalid, a JSON round trip, a camelCase mapping for an outside service, and a masked token. Next we leave plain data and write the first action. ## See also [Section titled “See also”](#see-also) * [Reserving places](/tutorial/reserving-places/): the next chapter * [JSON and serialization](/reference/field-struct/json-and-serialization/) * [Nested structs and arrays](/how-to/nested-structs-and-arrays/) * [Keep secrets out of logs](/how-to/hide-sensitive-values/)

# Composing actions

> Split the trek plan into small actions, use one as a step of another, undo work when a later step fails, and read the history.

`ReservePlaces` is about to grow. A trek also needs a shuttle to the trailhead and, now and then, a porter for the heavy packs. In this chapter we split the work into small actions and let a `PlanTrek` action run them in order. ## Where we left off [Section titled “Where we left off”](#where-we-left-off) We had one action that checked and took free places. Here that part stands alone, next to a table of shuttle times and a table of porter loads. small_actions.rb 
```ruby
FREE = { 'KR12' => 3 }
SHUTTLES = { 'north' => 35, 'east' => 80 }
PORTERS = { 'LIGHT' => 8, 'FULL' => 20 }


class TakePlaces < Actionable::Action
  input do
    required :hut_code, :string
    required :places, :integer
  end


  output do
    required :remaining, :integer
  end


  fails :fully_booked, 'not enough places free'


  step :check
  step :take


  private


  def check
    fail!(:fully_booked) if FREE.fetch(input.hut_code, 0) < input.places
  end


  def take
    FREE[input.hut_code] -= input.places
    @remaining = FREE[input.hut_code]
  end
end


class ChooseTrailhead < Actionable::Action
  input { required :valley, :string }
  output { required :shuttle_minutes, :integer }


  fails :no_shuttle, 'no shuttle serves that valley'


  step :pick


  private


  def pick
    minutes = SHUTTLES[input.valley]
    fail!(:no_shuttle) unless minutes
    @shuttle_minutes = minutes
  end
end


class AddPorter < Actionable::Action
  input { optional :porter_code, :string }
  output { optional :porter_kg, :integer }


  skips :no_porter


  step :assign


  private


  def assign
    skip!(:no_porter) if input.porter_code.nil?
    @porter_kg = PORTERS.fetch(input.porter_code, 0)
  end
end
```
 Each one runs alone, and each is easy to test. 
```ruby
ChooseTrailhead.run(valley: 'east').shuttle_minutes   # => 80
ChooseTrailhead.run(valley: 'west').code              # => :no_shuttle
AddPorter.run.skipped?                                # => true
```
 ## An action as a step [Section titled “An action as a step”](#an-action-as-a-step) A `step` can name an action class instead of a method. The parent passes values by naming instance variables with `input:`, and takes outputs back as instance variables, narrowed with `expose:`. plan_trek.rb 
```ruby
class PlanTrek < Actionable::Action
  input do
    required :hut_code, :string
    required :places, :integer
    required :valley, :string
    optional :porter, :string
  end


  output do
    required :remaining, :integer
    required :shuttle_minutes, :integer
    optional :porter_kg, :integer
  end


  fails :fully_booked, 'not enough places free'
  fails :no_shuttle, 'no shuttle serves that valley'


  step :prepare
  step TakePlaces, input: %i[hut_code places], expose: %i[remaining]
  step ChooseTrailhead, input: %i[valley], expose: %i[shuttle_minutes]
  step AddPorter, input: %i[porter_code], expose: %i[porter_kg]


  private


  def prepare
    @hut_code = input.hut_code
    @places = input.places
    @valley = input.valley
    @porter_code = input.porter
  end
end
```
 The `prepare` step copies the typed input into instance variables, since that’s what child steps read. Let’s run it. 
```ruby
done = PlanTrek.run(hut_code: 'KR12', places: 1, valley: 'east', porter: 'LIGHT')


done.success?           # => true
done.remaining          # => 2
done.shuttle_minutes    # => 80
done.porter_kg          # => 8
```
 ## A child skip does not stop the parent [Section titled “A child skip does not stop the parent”](#a-child-skip-does-not-stop-the-parent) With no porter requested, `AddPorter` skips. The parent carries on, as it would after a success. 
```ruby
plain = PlanTrek.run(hut_code: 'KR12', places: 1, valley: 'north')


plain.success?          # => true
plain.porter_kg         # => nil
plain.shuttle_minutes   # => 35
FREE['KR12']            # => 1
```
 A skip means “nothing to do here”. The parent doesn’t have to handle it. ## A child failure becomes the parent’s [Section titled “A child failure becomes the parent’s”](#a-child-failure-becomes-the-parents) Asking for more places than are free makes `TakePlaces` fail, and `PlanTrek` fails with the same code. 
```ruby
full = PlanTrek.run(hut_code: 'KR12', places: 5, valley: 'north')


full.failure?    # => true
full.code        # => :fully_booked
full.message     # => "not enough places free"
```
 Because `PlanTrek` declares failures, it has to declare every code its children can fail with. Leave out `:no_shuttle` and the first run raises `Actionable::DefinitionError`, naming the code. ## Undo with on_failure [Section titled “Undo with on_failure”](#undo-with-on_failure) Here is a real problem. When the valley has no shuttle, `TakePlaces` already took a place, and nobody can reach the hut. An `on_failure` hook runs after a failed run, so we give the place back. 
```ruby
class PlanTrek
  on_failure :release_places


  private


  def release_places
    FREE[@hut_code] += @places if @remaining
  end
end


before = FREE['KR12']
stuck = PlanTrek.run(hut_code: 'KR12', places: 1, valley: 'west')


stuck.code                  # => :no_shuttle
FREE['KR12'] == before      # => true
```
 The hook ran only because the run failed, and `@remaining` is set only when `TakePlaces` got far enough to succeed. After a `:fully_booked` failure nothing was taken, so there is nothing to give back. ## See what ran [Section titled “See what ran”](#see-what-ran) An action that measures keeps a history of every step. 
```ruby
class PlanTrek
  measure :all
end


traced = PlanTrek.run(hut_code: 'KR12', places: 1, valley: 'north')
traced.history.steps.size                           # => 4
traced.history.steps.map(&:section).uniq            # => [:main]
traced.history.took.class   # => Float
```
 The four entries are `prepare` and the three child actions, and no `:failure` entry shows up because nothing failed. Each entry names the section (`:main` for steps, `:failure` for the undo hook), the step and how long it took. Measuring is off by default, so a normal run pays nothing. ## What you built [Section titled “What you built”](#what-you-built) Three small actions and a `PlanTrek` that composes them. A child skip let the parent go on, a child failure became the parent’s, an `on_failure` hook returned the place, and `history` showed what ran. Next we take the trek planner into an app. ## See also [Section titled “See also”](#see-also) * [Into an app](/tutorial/into-an-app/): the next chapter * [Compose actions](/how-to/compose-actions/) * [Hooks and history](/reference/actionable/hooks-and-history/) * [Actions and steps](/reference/actionable/actions-and-steps/#nested-actions)

# Into an app

> Call an action from a controller and return JSON, handle a pass-report lookup that times out, retry it in a job and write a spec.

The trek planner works in a console. In this last chapter we put it behind a web endpoint: a controller that returns JSON, a pass-report lookup that sometimes times out, a background job that retries it, and a spec. ## Where we left off [Section titled “Where we left off”](#where-we-left-off) We had a plan built from small actions. Here is a slimmer version, with a lookup that asks another service how much snow lies on a pass. The service is a stand-in object, since the tutorial never touches the network. While `PassReport.down` is true the lookup raises `Timeout::Error`, the way a slow HTTP call would. plan_trek.rb 
```ruby
require 'timeout'
require 'json'


FREE = { 'KR12' => 5 }


module PassReport
  class << self
    attr_accessor :down


    def snow_cm_for(pass)
      raise Timeout::Error, 'pass report timed out' if down


      { 'Larch' => 12, 'Eagle' => 40 }[pass]
    end
  end
end


class CheckPass < Actionable::Action
  input { required :pass, :string }
  output { required :snow_cm, :integer }


  fails :unknown_pass, 'no report covers that pass', retry: false
  fails :report_timeout, 'the pass report did not answer', retry: true


  step :look_up


  private


  def look_up
    depth = PassReport.snow_cm_for(input.pass)
    fail!(:unknown_pass) unless depth
    @snow_cm = depth
  rescue Timeout::Error
    fail!(:report_timeout)
  end
end


class PlanTrek < Actionable::Action
  input do
    required :hut_code, :string
    required :places, :integer, in: 1..10
    required :pass, :string
  end


  output do
    required :snow_cm, :integer
  end


  fails :fully_booked, 'not enough places free', retry: false
  fails :unknown_pass, 'no report covers that pass', retry: false
  fails :report_timeout, 'the pass report did not answer', retry: true


  step :check_availability
  step :prepare
  step CheckPass, input: %i[pass], expose: %i[snow_cm]
  step :reserve


  private


  def check_availability
    fail!(:fully_booked) if FREE.fetch(input.hut_code, 0) < input.places
  end


  def prepare
    @pass = input.pass
  end


  def reserve
    FREE[input.hut_code] -= input.places
  end
end
```
 The lookup turns the exception it expects into a named failure, `:report_timeout`, with `retry: true` in its metadata. An exception it doesn’t expect still propagates, as chapter 4 showed. ## Call it from a controller [Section titled “Call it from a controller”](#call-it-from-a-controller) A controller turns params into an action call and a result into a response. This one is a plain class so the example runs anywhere. In Rails the shape is the same, with `params.to_unsafe_h` or a permitted hash where we use a plain one. treks_controller.rb 
```ruby
class TreksController
  STATUS = { fully_booked: 409, unknown_pass: 422, report_timeout: 503, invalid_input: 422 }.freeze


  def create(params)
    result = PlanTrek.run(**params)


    case result
    in Actionable::Success
      [201, JSON.generate(snow_cm: result.snow_cm)]
    in Actionable::Failure
      [STATUS.fetch(result.code, 500), JSON.generate(error: result.code, message: result.message, fields: result.errors.to_h)]
    end
  end
end


controller = TreksController.new


status, body = controller.create(hut_code: 'KR12', places: '1', pass: 'Eagle')
status                               # => 201
JSON.parse(body)                     # => {"snow_cm" => 40}


status, body = controller.create(hut_code: 'KR12', places: '1', pass: 'Nowhere')
status                               # => 422
JSON.parse(body)['error']            # => "unknown_pass"


status, body = controller.create(hut_code: 'KR12', places: '99', pass: 'Larch')
status                               # => 422
JSON.parse(body)['fields']           # => {"places" => ["is invalid"]}
```
 Params go in with `**`. A single positional hash is ignored, and every field would read as missing. ## A collaborator that times out [Section titled “A collaborator that times out”](#a-collaborator-that-times-out) Now we make the pass report slow. 
```ruby
PassReport.down = true


status, body = TreksController.new.create(hut_code: 'KR12', places: '1', pass: 'Eagle')
status                               # => 503
JSON.parse(body)['error']            # => "report_timeout"
FREE['KR12']                         # => 4
```
 The controller answered 503, which tells a client to try again later. The action didn’t blow up, and the response was built from a value. The free count is still 4, which is what the earlier successful plan left. The lookup runs before `reserve`, so the timeout took nothing, and that is what makes `retry: true` honest. A failure that carries it is safe to run again. ## Retry in a job [Section titled “Retry in a job”](#retry-in-a-job) A timeout is worth another try and an unknown pass is not. The metadata we declared says which is which. `actionable/job` turns a result into `:ack`, `:discard` or `:retry`, and the mixin does it inside your job class. plan_trek_job.rb 
```ruby
require 'actionable/job'


class PlanTrek
  honor_retry_metadata
end


class PlanTrekJob
  include Actionable::Job::Mixin


  def perform(**params)
    run_actionable(PlanTrek, **params)
  end
end


job = PlanTrekJob.new
trek = { hut_code: 'KR12', places: 1, pass: 'Eagle' }


begin
  job.perform(**trek)
rescue Actionable::Job::RetryableFailure => e
  e.result.code    # => :report_timeout
end
```
 While the service is down, `perform` raises `RetryableFailure`. Your queue sees an exception and retries on its own schedule. With ActiveJob you add `retry_on Actionable::Job::RetryableFailure` to the class, and Sidekiq retries any raise by default. Once the service is back, a retry succeeds. An `:unknown_pass` failure comes back as a result instead, so the job finishes and the queue forgets it. 
```ruby
PassReport.down = false


job.perform(**trek, pass: 'Nowhere').code   # => :unknown_pass
job.perform(**trek).success?                # => true
FREE['KR12']                                # => 3
```
 ## Test it [Section titled “Test it”](#test-it) The `perform_actionable` matcher runs an action and checks its outcome in one line. In a real suite you require `actionable/rspec` and write `expect(...).to perform_actionable(...)`. The matcher also works on its own, so here we check it on a small stand-in for an example group. 
```ruby
require 'actionable/rspec'


spec = Object.new.extend(Actionable::RSpec::Matchers)


spec.perform_actionable(hut_code: 'KR12', places: 1, pass: 'Larch').matches?(PlanTrek)                                  # => true
spec.perform_actionable(hut_code: 'KR12', places: 1, pass: 'Nowhere').and_fail(:unknown_pass).matches?(PlanTrek)        # => true
spec.perform_actionable(hut_code: 'KR12', places: 99, pass: 'Larch').and_fail(:invalid_input).matches?(PlanTrek)        # => true
```
 Specs for the timeout shouldn’t lean on a real slow service. Stub the child action and the parent sees the failure you chose. These examples need RSpec’s mocks, so they’re plain code here. 
```ruby
require 'actionable/rspec'


RSpec.describe PlanTrek do
  it 'reports a pass-report timeout' do
    allow_actionable_failure(CheckPass, code: :report_timeout)


    expect(PlanTrek).to perform_actionable(hut_code: 'KR12', places: 1, pass: 'Eagle')
      .and_fail(:report_timeout)
  end


  it 'carries the retry metadata' do
    allow_actionable_failure(CheckPass, code: :report_timeout)


    result = PlanTrek.run(hut_code: 'KR12', places: 1, pass: 'Eagle')
    expect(result.metadata).to eq(retry: true)
  end
end
```
 `allow_actionable_failure` builds a real `Failure`, with the declared message and metadata for that code, so the stub can’t drift from the action. Asking for a code the action doesn’t declare raises in the spec itself. ## What you built [Section titled “What you built”](#what-you-built) A trek planner from `Hut` to a job and a spec: typed structs, JSON, actions with named outcomes, composition with an undo, a controller that maps codes to statuses, a flaky lookup turned into a retryable failure, and specs that don’t need the network. From here, pick what you need. ## See also [Section titled “See also”](#see-also) * [How-to guides](/how-to/): one task each, from validating params to RuboCop * [Run in a background job](/how-to/background-jobs/) * [Test with RSpec](/how-to/test-with-rspec/) * [Reference](/reference/): every option and method

# Reserving places

> Write the first action, with typed input and output, a declared fully_booked failure and a branch on the result.

So far we’ve only described data. Now something has to happen: a party reserves places in a hut. In this chapter we write our first action, give it typed input and output, make it fail on purpose, and branch on the result. ## Where we left off [Section titled “Where we left off”](#where-we-left-off) We had `Hut`, `Leg` and `Booking` structs. This chapter also needs a count of free places per hut, so a plain hash stands in for a database. free_places.rb 
```ruby
FREE = { 'KR12' => 3, 'EG07' => 0 }
```
 ## An action with steps [Section titled “An action with steps”](#an-action-with-steps) An action is a class that inherits from `Actionable::Action`. Each `step` names an instance method, and the steps run in the order written. We start one with `.run`. first_reservation.rb 
```ruby
class FirstReservation < Actionable::Action
  step :hold
  step :confirm


  private


  def hold
    @held = true
  end


  def confirm
    succeed('places reserved')
  end
end


result = FirstReservation.run
result.success?   # => true
result.message    # => "places reserved"
```
 `.run` always hands back a result, never `nil`. `succeed` records a success with a message and ends the run there. ## Typed input [Section titled “Typed input”](#typed-input) A reservation needs a contact, a hut code and a number of places. An `input` block is a FieldStruct body, so everything from chapter 1 applies: types, options and coercion. 
```ruby
class TypedReservation < Actionable::Action
  input do
    required :contact, :email
    required :hut_code, :string, format: /\A[A-Z]{2}\d{2}\z/
    required :places, :integer, in: 1..10
  end


  step :confirm


  private


  def confirm
    succeed("reserving #{input.places}")
  end
end
```
 Steps read the validated struct through `input`, and the count can arrive as a string. 
```ruby
TypedReservation.run(contact: 'lead@example.com', hut_code: 'KR12', places: '2').message   # => "reserving 2"
```
 ## Bad input never reaches a step [Section titled “Bad input never reaches a step”](#bad-input-never-reaches-a-step) What if someone asks for 99 places? We write no code for that. 
```ruby
bad = TypedReservation.run(contact: 'lead@example.com', hut_code: 'KR12', places: 99)


bad.failure?              # => true
bad.code                  # => :invalid_input
bad.errors.to_h           # => {places: ["is invalid"]}
```
 Invalid input ends the run as a failure with the code `:invalid_input` and the struct’s errors. No step ran. ## Fail on purpose [Section titled “Fail on purpose”](#fail-on-purpose) Now the real action. Asking for more places than are free is a business failure, not bad input. We declare it with `fails` and end the run with `fail!`. The availability check goes first, so a failure stops the run before `reserve` touches anything. reserve_places.rb 
```ruby
class ReservePlaces < Actionable::Action
  input do
    required :contact, :email
    required :hut_code, :string, format: /\A[A-Z]{2}\d{2}\z/
    required :places, :integer, in: 1..10
  end


  fails :fully_booked, 'not enough places free'


  step :check_availability
  step :reserve


  private


  def check_availability
    fail!(:fully_booked) if FREE.fetch(input.hut_code, 0) < input.places
  end


  def reserve
    FREE[input.hut_code] -= input.places
    succeed("reserved #{input.places}")
  end
end
```
 `fail!` records the failure and stops the run, so `reserve` never executes. The plain `fail` records and keeps going, which is almost never what you want in a step. Caution A plain `fail`, `succeed` or `skip` has to be the last thing a method does, because code after it still runs. The first `.run` of a class raises and names the line if a method gets this wrong. 
```ruby
full = ReservePlaces.run(contact: 'lead@example.com', hut_code: 'KR12', places: 4)


full.failure?      # => true
full.code          # => :fully_booked
full.message       # => "not enough places free"
FREE['KR12']       # => 3
```
 The message came from the declaration and the free count didn’t change. Once an action declares a failure it can only fail with declared codes, so a typo like `:fully_bookd` raises on the first run instead of turning into a result nobody handles. ## A declared output [Section titled “A declared output”](#a-declared-output) A success should hand something back. We reopen the class, add an `output` block, and set an instance variable of the same name in `reserve`. 
```ruby
class ReservePlaces
  output do
    required :remaining, :integer
  end


  private


  def reserve
    FREE[input.hut_code] -= input.places
    @remaining = FREE[input.hut_code]
  end
end


done = ReservePlaces.run(contact: 'lead@example.com', hut_code: 'KR12', places: 2)
done.success?    # => true
done.remaining   # => 1
done.code        # => :success
```
 Only declared output fields reach the result, so nothing leaks by accident. There was no `succeed` this time, because a run with no recorded outcome succeeds on its own. ## Branch on the result [Section titled “Branch on the result”](#branch-on-the-result) Calling code asks the result what happened. 
```ruby
def report(**params)
  result = ReservePlaces.run(**params)
  if result.success?
    "reserved, #{result.remaining} free"
  elsif result.code == :fully_booked
    'sorry, no room'
  else
    "check the request: #{result.errors.to_h.keys.join(', ')}"
  end
end


report(contact: 'lead@example.com', hut_code: 'KR12', places: 1)    # => "reserved, 0 free"
report(contact: 'lead@example.com', hut_code: 'KR12', places: 1)    # => "sorry, no room"
report(contact: 'nope', hut_code: 'KR12', places: 1)                # => "check the request: contact"
```
 We never rescued anything. All three outcomes are values. ## What you built [Section titled “What you built”](#what-you-built) A `ReservePlaces` action with typed input, a declared failure, a declared output and a caller that branches on `success?` and `code`. Bad input turned into `:invalid_input` before any step ran. Next we meet the third outcome, the skip, and find out what a real exception is. ## See also [Section titled “See also”](#see-also) * [When things go wrong](/tutorial/when-things-go-wrong/): the next chapter * [Actions and steps](/reference/actionable/actions-and-steps/) * [Input and output](/reference/actionable/input-and-output/) * [Declare and handle outcomes](/how-to/handle-action-outcomes/)

# Plan a trek across a hut network

> A six-chapter tutorial that builds a trek planner for a network of mountain huts with field_struct and actionable, one step at a time.

In this tutorial we build the booking side of a mountain hut network. We start with a typed `Hut`, nest it into bookings and JSON, write the action that reserves places, handle what goes wrong, split the work into small actions, and put the result behind a controller, a job and a spec. Checked against `field_struct` 1.0.0.pre1 and `actionable-rb` 2.0.0.pre1, the versions in this repository. ## The six chapters [Section titled “The six chapters”](#the-six-chapters) | Chapter | We make | Gem | | ---------------------------------------------------------- | ---------------------------------------------------------- | ------------ | | [1. A typed Hut](/tutorial/a-typed-hut/) | a `Hut` with typed, coerced, validated fields | field_struct | | [2. Bookings and JSON](/tutorial/bookings-and-json/) | nested bookings, JSON round trips, a masked token | field_struct | | [3. Reserving places](/tutorial/reserving-places/) | the first action, with typed input and a declared failure | actionable | | [4. When things go wrong](/tutorial/when-things-go-wrong/) | skips, metadata, pattern matching, exceptions | actionable | | [5. Composing actions](/tutorial/composing-actions/) | a trek plan built from small actions, with an undo | actionable | | [6. Into an app](/tutorial/into-an-app/) | a controller, a flaky pass-report lookup, a job and a spec | actionable | Each chapter takes about 20 minutes, and chapters 1 and 2 need only `field_struct`. ## What you need [Section titled “What you need”](#what-you-need) Ruby 3.3 or newer, and these two gems: 
```ruby
gem 'field_struct', '~> 1.0.0.pre1'
gem 'actionable-rb', '~> 2.0.0.pre1'
```
 ## How to follow along [Section titled “How to follow along”](#how-to-follow-along) Type each example into `irb`, or paste a whole chapter into one script. Begin with these two lines. 
```ruby
require 'field_struct'
require 'actionable'
```
 Every chapter declares the classes it needs again, so you can start at any of them. Inside a chapter the examples build on each other, in order. A line like `hut.places # => 24` shows the value that expression returns. All of them are checked: the site’s build runs every example in order and fails when a result differs from its comment, so the code you read is code that ran. Where an example needs a web server, a queue or RSpec, the tutorial uses a plain Ruby stand-in or a plain code block, and says so. ## Where to go after [Section titled “Where to go after”](#where-to-go-after) * [How-to guides](/how-to/): one task each * [Reference](/reference/): every option and method * [Explanation](/explanation/): why the gems are shaped this way ## See also [Section titled “See also”](#see-also) * [A typed Hut](/tutorial/a-typed-hut/): chapter 1 * [How the two gems fit together](/explanation/how-the-gems-fit/) * [Reference overview](/reference/)

# When things go wrong

> Meet the skip, attach metadata to a failure, match on results, read errors as data and see why exceptions are not outcomes.

An action ends one of three ways: success, failure or skip. This chapter covers the two that aren’t success, plus the one thing that isn’t an outcome at all, a real exception. ## Where we left off [Section titled “Where we left off”](#where-we-left-off) We had `ReservePlaces`, which takes free places from a hut and fails with `:fully_booked`. We declare it again, this time with a booking id so a booking can be “already handled”. reserve_places.rb 
```ruby
FREE = { 'KR12' => 3 }
HANDLED = []


class ReservePlaces < Actionable::Action
  input do
    required :booking_id, :integer
    required :hut_code, :string, format: /\A[A-Z]{2}\d{2}\z/
    required :places, :integer, in: 1..10
  end


  output do
    required :remaining, :integer
  end


  fails :fully_booked, 'not enough places free'
  skips :already_reserved, 'this booking was handled before'


  step :check_handled
  step :check_availability
  step :reserve


  private


  def check_handled
    skip!(:already_reserved) if HANDLED.include?(input.booking_id)
  end


  def check_availability
    fail!(:fully_booked) if FREE.fetch(input.hut_code, 0) < input.places
  end


  def reserve
    FREE[input.hut_code] -= input.places
    HANDLED << input.booking_id
    @remaining = FREE[input.hut_code]
  end
end
```
 ## A skip is not a failure [Section titled “A skip is not a failure”](#a-skip-is-not-a-failure) Handling booking 7 twice should not take places twice, and it isn’t an error either. There was simply nothing left to do. That is a skip. 
```ruby
first = ReservePlaces.run(booking_id: 7, hut_code: 'KR12', places: 1)
again = ReservePlaces.run(booking_id: 7, hut_code: 'KR12', places: 1)


first.success?       # => true
again.skipped?       # => true
again.code           # => :already_reserved
again.message        # => "this booking was handled before"
again.failure?       # => false
again.ok?            # => true
FREE['KR12']         # => 2
```
 `ok?` is true for a success and for a skip, and false only for a failure, so a retry loop can ask `ok?` and stop. Declaring `skips` works like `fails`: the codes are a contract and only declared ones are allowed. ## Metadata on a failure [Section titled “Metadata on a failure”](#metadata-on-a-failure) A failure can carry facts for whoever handles it. We mark `:fully_booked` as worth retrying later, since cancellations free places up. 
```ruby
class ReservePlaces
  fails :fully_booked, 'not enough places free', retry: true
end


late = ReservePlaces.run(booking_id: 8, hut_code: 'KR12', places: 5)
late.code        # => :fully_booked
late.metadata    # => {retry: true}
```
 The gem doesn’t act on `retry:`. It is data you declare once and read where you need it, and chapter 6 reads it. ## Match on results [Section titled “Match on results”](#match-on-results) Results work with `case/in`. The class says which outcome it was and the code says why. 
```ruby
def describe_result(result)
  case result
  in Actionable::Success                            then "reserved, #{result.remaining} free"
  in Actionable::Skipped[code: :already_reserved]   then 'already done'
  in Actionable::Failure[code: :fully_booked]       then 'no room'
  in Actionable::Failure[code: :invalid_input]      then 'bad request'
  end
end


describe_result(ReservePlaces.run(booking_id: 9, hut_code: 'KR12', places: 1))    # => "reserved, 1 free"
describe_result(ReservePlaces.run(booking_id: 9, hut_code: 'KR12', places: 1))    # => "already done"
describe_result(ReservePlaces.run(booking_id: 10, hut_code: 'KR12', places: 5))   # => "no room"
describe_result(ReservePlaces.run(booking_id: 11, hut_code: 'KR12', places: 0))   # => "bad request"
```
 With no `else`, a result that no branch matches raises `NoMatchingPatternError`. In a controller that is often what you want, since a code you forgot about then fails loudly. ## Errors as data [Section titled “Errors as data”](#errors-as-data) A failure keeps its errors as data, so you can hand them to a person or to an API client. 
```ruby
bad = ReservePlaces.run(booking_id: 12, hut_code: 'k1', places: 0)


bad.code                        # => :invalid_input
bad.errors.to_h.keys            # => [:hut_code, :places]
bad.errors.full_messages.size   # => 2
bad.to_h.keys                   # => [:code, :message, :output, :history, :errors, :metadata]
bad.to_api_h(index: 0)          # => {index: 0, status: :failure, id: nil, errors: {hut_code: ["is invalid"], places: ["is invalid"]}}
```
 `to_h` is the whole result as a hash. `to_api_h` is the small shape a batch API wants: an index, a status, an id and the errors. ## An exception is not an outcome [Section titled “An exception is not an outcome”](#an-exception-is-not-an-outcome) Everything so far was a result. A real bug is something else. 
```ruby
class Broken < Actionable::Action
  step :explode


  private


  def explode
    raise 'the availability service is down'
  end
end


begin
  Broken.run
rescue RuntimeError => e
  e.message   # => "the availability service is down"
end
```
 The exception goes straight through `.run`. It is neither a failure nor a skip, so there is no result to branch on. That is deliberate: a failure is something your code expected and named, and an exception is something it didn’t. Your app’s error handler, or your job queue’s retry rules, take it from there. ## What you built [Section titled “What you built”](#what-you-built) An action that ends as a success, a skip or a failure, a failure with metadata, a `case/in` that handles each outcome, errors as data in `to_h` and `to_api_h`, and a clear line between named outcomes and exceptions. Next we split the trip planning into small actions that call each other. ## See also [Section titled “See also”](#see-also) * [Composing actions](/tutorial/composing-actions/): the next chapter * [Results](/reference/actionable/results/) * [Success, failure, skip](/explanation/three-outcomes/) * [Declare and handle outcomes](/how-to/handle-action-outcomes/)

# Adapters

> Optional gems that export field_struct classes to JSON Schema, Avro and Protobuf, trace actionable with OpenTelemetry, and expose actions as LLM tools.

An adapter is an optional gem on top of `field_struct` or `actionable`, and it handles one format. None is released yet, so each example here runs against the code in this repository. Unreleased All the adapters are at `1.0.0.pre1`. Protobuf and tools are previews. Their specs settle the behaviour, but none of it has shipped. ## Choose an adapter [Section titled “Choose an adapter”](#choose-an-adapter) | You want to | Adapter | Gem | | --------------------------------------------------------------- | ----------------------------------------------- | -------------------------- | | publish a struct’s JSON as a schema, for docs or other services | [JSON Schema](/adapters/json-schema/) | `field_struct-json_schema` | | write structs as Avro binary and read old events | [Avro](/adapters/avro/) | `field_struct-avro` | | encode Avro faster, with an optional Rust encoder | [Avro native encoder](/adapters/avro-native/) | `field_struct-avro-native` | | print a `.proto` and encode structs with Protobuf | [Protobuf](/adapters/protobuf/) (preview) | `field_struct-protobuf` | | trace action runs | [OpenTelemetry](/adapters/opentelemetry/) | `actionable-otel` | | let a language model call an action | [LLM and MCP tools](/adapters/tools/) (preview) | `actionable-tools` | ## Install [Section titled “Install”](#install) Every adapter is a separate gem, and you load it with an explicit `require`. 
```ruby
gem 'field_struct-json_schema'   # require 'field_struct/json_schema'
gem 'field_struct-avro'          # require 'field_struct/avro'
gem 'field_struct-protobuf'      # require 'field_struct/protobuf'
gem 'actionable-otel'            # require 'actionable/otel'
gem 'actionable-tools'           # require 'actionable/tools'
```
 Some pull in a library and some leave that to you. Avro depends on `avro` and Protobuf on `google-protobuf`. OpenTelemetry depends on `opentelemetry-api` alone, so the SDK and the exporter are your choice. The tools adapter needs `mcp` or `ruby_llm` only for the wrapper you require. ## How they behave [Section titled “How they behave”](#how-they-behave) * **Module functions first.** Each adapter works as plain functions, like `FieldStruct::Avro.schema(Hut)`, and has an opt-in `Methods` module for when you want `Hut.to_avro_schema` on the class. * **Requiring leaves your classes alone.** OpenTelemetry is the exception: `require 'actionable/otel'` turns tracing on. * **One shape, many formats.** The three format adapters share a single walk over a struct’s fields, [`FieldStruct::Introspection`](/adapters/introspection/). Output names come from `serialize`, so one declaration can call a field something different in `:json`, `:avro` and `:protobuf`. * **They refuse rather than approximate.** If a format can’t hold something exactly, the adapter raises, or leaves it out and documents that. 
```ruby
require 'field_struct/json_schema'


class Hut < FieldStruct::Base
  required :name, :string
end


Hut.respond_to?(:to_json_schema)                  # => false
FieldStruct::JSONSchema.generate(Hut)['required'] # => ["name"]


class Hut
  include FieldStruct::JSONSchema::Methods
end


Hut.respond_to?(:to_json_schema)                  # => true
```
 ## Go further [Section titled “Go further”](#go-further) * [How adapters work](/adapters/how-adapters-work/): why they are separate gems, and why they never approximate * [Introspection reference](/adapters/introspection/): the walk all the format adapters share * [Build your own adapter](/adapters/build-your-own/): a generator for another format in about 60 lines ## See also [Section titled “See also”](#see-also) * [How the two gems fit together](/explanation/how-the-gems-fit/) * [Introspection and tooling](/reference/field-struct/introspection-and-tooling/)

# Avro

> Write structs as Avro binary, read dispatches saved with an older class, and get an error where a value would have been rounded.

`field_struct-avro` maps a struct to an Avro record schema, an instance to a datum or to Avro binary, and bytes back to an instance. That includes bytes an older version of the class wrote. 
```ruby
gem 'field_struct-avro'
```
 
```ruby
require 'field_struct/avro'
```
 The unit of work is one datum. Container files, a schema registry and Avro JSON are all out of scope, and so is validation (`format:`, `in:`), which stays with the struct. If Avro has no exact way to hold a value, you get an `ArgumentError` instead of a rounded number. ## The schema of a struct [Section titled “The schema of a struct”](#the-schema-of-a-struct) 
```ruby
require 'field_struct/avro'


module Signals
  class Dispatch < FieldStruct::Base
    required :id, :uuid
    required :lamp, :symbol, enum: %i[fixed flashing]
    required :swell, :big_decimal, round: 2
    required :sent, :time, zone: 'America/Denver'
    optional :channels, :array, of: :string
  end
end


schema = FieldStruct::Avro.schema(Signals::Dispatch)
schema['name']                  # => "Dispatch"
schema['namespace']             # => "Signals"
schema['fields'].map { |f| f['name'] }   # => ["id", "lamp", "swell", "sent", "channels"]
schema['fields'][1]['type']     # => {"type"=>"enum", "name"=>"Dispatch_lamp", "symbols"=>["fixed", "flashing"]}
schema['fields'][2]['type']     # => {"type"=>"bytes", "logicalType"=>"decimal", "precision"=>38, "scale"=>2}
schema['fields'][4]['type']     # => ["null", {"type"=>"array", "items"=>["null", "string"]}]
```
 The mapping holds on to as much as Avro allows. Integers become `long`, never `int`. A decimal that has a scale (from `round:`) becomes an Avro `decimal`, and times keep every sub-second digit. A symbol with `enum:` turns into an Avro `enum`. Optional fields are unions with `null`. Array items are always nullable too, because a struct lets `nil` elements through. | Field type | Avro | | ------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------- | | `:integer` | `long` | | `:float` | `double` | | `:string`, `:email`, `:url`, `:value` | `string` (`:value` holds JSON text) | | `:uuid` | `string` with `logicalType` `uuid` | | `:binary` | `bytes` | | `:big_decimal` | `decimal` with the field’s scale, or `string` with no scale | | `:date` | `int` with `logicalType` `date` | | `:time`, `:datetime` | `long` with `timestamp-micros`, `-nanos` or `-millis` | | `:symbol` with `enum:` | a named `enum`; otherwise `string` | | arrays | `array`, items nullable | | nested structs | a named `record`, referenced by full name after its first use | A field with a usable default is not nullable, and the schema carries that default. A default Avro can’t write (a date, a decimal, a lambda) stays out of the schema, and the struct fills it in on read. Each `schema` call gives you a fresh copy. For `Signals::Dispatch.to_avro_schema`, `#to_avro` and `Signals::Dispatch.from_avro`, include `FieldStruct::Avro::Methods`. ## Use case: send a signal, read it back [Section titled “Use case: send a signal, read it back”](#use-case-send-a-signal-read-it-back) Say a lighthouse sends a dispatch to the harbor office. `encode` writes one datum as Avro binary, and `decode` reads it into a struct of whichever class you name. 
```ruby
dispatch = Signals::Dispatch.new(id: '3c5e7a10-92b4-4f6d-8e21-0a7d5b9c1e44', lamp: :flashing, swell: '2.75',
                                 sent: '2026-07-01T08:00:00', channels: %w[ch-16 ch-9])


bytes = FieldStruct::Avro.encode(dispatch)
bytes.encoding                                # => #<Encoding:BINARY (ASCII-8BIT)>
back = FieldStruct::Avro.decode(Signals::Dispatch, bytes)
back == dispatch                              # => true
back.sent.utc_offset                          # => -21600
```
 The field’s `zone:` puts the offset back on the way in. When all you need is the Hash the `avro` gem reads and writes, call `to_datum` and `from_datum`. 
```ruby
datum = FieldStruct::Avro.to_datum(dispatch)
datum.keys                                                       # => ["id", "lamp", "swell", "sent", "channels"]
FieldStruct::Avro.from_datum(Signals::Dispatch, datum) == dispatch   # => true
```
 ## Use case: read old signals after a class changes [Section titled “Use case: read old signals after a class changes”](#use-case-read-old-signals-after-a-class-changes) A dispatch logged last month predates two fields the class has since gained. Hand `decode` the schema those bytes were written with, as `writer_schema:`. A Hash, a JSON String or a parsed `Avro::Schema` all work. Avro pairs records by name, so the old schema must carry the namespace of the class doing the reading. 
```ruby
module Gen1
  class Buoy < FieldStruct::Base
    required :serial, :integer
  end
end


module Gen2
  class Buoy < FieldStruct::Base
    required :serial, :integer
    optional :callsign, :string
    required :moored, :date, default: Date.new(2026, 3, 14)
  end
end


old_bytes = FieldStruct::Avro.encode(Gen1::Buoy.new(serial: 31))
old_schema = FieldStruct::Avro.schema(Gen1::Buoy).merge('namespace' => 'Gen2')


buoy = FieldStruct::Avro.decode(Gen2::Buoy, old_bytes, writer_schema: old_schema)
[buoy.serial, buoy.callsign, buoy.moored]   # => [31, nil, Date.new(2026, 3, 14)]
```
 A field the writer never had gets the struct’s own default, even one Avro can’t express, like a date. ## It raises rather than lose data [Section titled “It raises rather than lose data”](#it-raises-rather-than-lose-data) A time carrying nanoseconds does not fit `timestamp-micros`. Rounding would change the value, so the adapter stops and tells you what to do about it. 
```ruby
class Roster < FieldStruct::Base
  required :keeper_name, :string
  required :logged, :time
  serialize :avro, keeper_name: 'keeperName'
end


entry = Roster.new(keeper_name: 'Odd', logged: Time.utc(2026, 10, 25, 21, 55, 12, Rational(123_456_789, 1000)))


begin
  FieldStruct::Avro.encode(entry)
rescue ArgumentError => e
  e.message   # => "FieldStruct::Avro: Roster#logged is 2026-10-25T21:55:12.123456789Z, which has more precision than timestamp-micros; round it, or use timestamp: :nanos"
end


nanos = FieldStruct::Avro.encode(entry, timestamp: :nanos)
FieldStruct::Avro.decode(Roster, nanos, timestamp: :nanos) == entry   # => true
FieldStruct::Avro.to_datum(entry, timestamp: :nanos).keys             # => ["keeperName", "logged"]
```
 In the datum, the `serialize :avro` mapping renamed `keeper_name` to `keeperName`. `serialization:` defaults to `:avro`. Reading gets the same care. The `avro` gem matches fields on their base type alone, so a decimal read at a different scale would come out as a wrong number with no warning. The adapter compares the two schemas first and refuses the decode, listing every problem it found. 
```ruby
module Spring
  class Tide < FieldStruct::Base
    required :height, :big_decimal, round: 2
  end
end


module Autumn
  class Tide < FieldStruct::Base
    required :height, :big_decimal, round: 3
  end
end


logged = FieldStruct::Avro.encode(Spring::Tide.new(height: '4.25'))
written_with = FieldStruct::Avro.schema(Spring::Tide).merge('namespace' => 'Autumn')


begin
  FieldStruct::Avro.decode(Autumn::Tide, logged, writer_schema: written_with)
rescue ArgumentError => e
  e.message   # => "FieldStruct::Avro can't read these bytes: height: written as decimal (scale 2), read as decimal (scale 3). The avro gem would decode them as the wrong value, or fail; this is not a compatible schema change"
end
```
 Every error is an `ArgumentError` naming the class and field, and none of them echoes the value of a `sensitive: true` field. Bytes that can’t be read at all raise a single `ArgumentError`, with the `avro` gem’s own error as its `cause`. ## Options [Section titled “Options”](#options) A field’s `avro:` option is a Hash. With `type:` you replace the whole mapping by an Avro type of your own, and the other keys adjust the generated one. On array and union fields, every key except `type:` applies to the items. | Key | Meaning | | ---------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------ | | `type:` | an Avro type (Hash or String) used as is | | `timestamp:` | `:micros`, `:nanos` or `:millis` for this field | | `precision:`, `scale:` | the decimal’s precision and scale | | `enum: false` | write a symbol field as `string` | | `enum_default:` | the symbol to read when the writer used one the reader lacks | 
```ruby
class Reading < FieldStruct::Base
  required :depth, :big_decimal, avro: { scale: 3, precision: 10 }
  required :taken, :time, avro: { timestamp: :millis }
  required :state, :symbol, enum: %i[calm rough], avro: { enum_default: :calm }
  required :station, :string, avro: { type: { 'type' => 'string', 'x-origin' => 'gauge' } }
end


types = FieldStruct::Avro.schema(Reading)['fields'].to_h { |f| [f['name'], f['type']] }
types['depth']    # => {"type"=>"bytes", "logicalType"=>"decimal", "precision"=>10, "scale"=>3}
types['taken']    # => {"type"=>"long", "logicalType"=>"timestamp-millis"}
types['state']    # => {"type"=>"enum", "name"=>"Reading_state", "symbols"=>["calm", "rough"], "default"=>"calm"}
types['station']  # => {"type"=>"string", "x-origin"=>"gauge"}
```
 Every module function accepts `serialization:`, and the ones that write or read times accept `timestamp:` as well. | Call | Returns | | ------------------------------------------------------------------ | ------------------------------------------ | | `schema(klass, serialization:, timestamp:)` | the record schema, a Hash with String keys | | `to_datum(struct, serialization:, timestamp:)` | the datum Hash | | `from_datum(klass, datum, serialization:)` | a struct | | `encode(struct, serialization:, timestamp:)` | Avro binary | | `decode(klass, bytes, writer_schema:, serialization:, timestamp:)` | a struct | | `clear_cache` | `nil` | ## Limits [Section titled “Limits”](#limits) * When the writer used a symbol that the reader’s `enum:` lacks, the read raises and names the field, unless that field sets `avro: {enum_default:}`. * A handful of errors come straight from the `avro` gem and may quote the value: a decimal past its precision, an integer past 64 bits, or a value that does not fit an `avro: {type:}` override. * Schemas are cached per class. The cache sees a new or redeclared field, but not an `avro:` Hash or `enum:` Array edited in place, so redeclare the field. Use `clear_cache` when code reloads. ## See also [Section titled “See also”](#see-also) * [Introspection reference](/adapters/introspection/) * [How adapters work](/adapters/how-adapters-work/) * [Dates, times and zones](/reference/field-struct/dates-and-times/)

# Avro native encoder

> An optional Rust encoder for field_struct-avro: the same Avro bytes, written about 2.6 times faster, with the pure Ruby path as the fallback.

`field_struct-avro-native` is an optional speed-up for [`FieldStruct::Avro.encode`](/adapters/avro/). It writes the same bytes as the pure Ruby path, from Rust, and anything it isn’t sure about goes back to Ruby. `field_struct-avro` never depends on it. 
```ruby
gem 'field_struct-avro-native'
```
 
```ruby
require 'field_struct/avro/native'
```
 Without that `require`, nothing changes. ## What it speeds up [Section titled “What it speeds up”](#what-it-speeds-up) `encode` builds a datum with `to_datum`, then hands it to the `avro` gem’s binary writer. The writer is most of the cost. It validates the whole datum first, then writes varints one byte at a time. The native gem replaces only that last step. It compiles each writer schema once into an encoding plan and walks the datum in Rust into a single buffer. The numbers below are in `benchmarks/BASELINE.md` (“Native avro encoder”), measured on Ruby 4.0.7 with YJIT, the Order-like struct from `field_struct-avro`’s benchmark: | Workload | Pure Ruby | Native | Speedup | | -------------------------------- | ----------------- | ----------------- | ------- | | `encode`, 1 order | 23,476 per second | 61,468 per second | 2.62x | | `encode`, 1000 orders (per call) | 23.2 | 61.6 | 2.66x | | Objects allocated per order | 417 | 155 | | The write step alone goes from 26.4 us to 1.9 us, about 14 times faster. What remains is mostly `to_datum`, at about 12.6 us. Reading is untouched: `decode` stays pure Ruby. ## Turn it on [Section titled “Turn it on”](#turn-it-on) Require it once, after `field_struct/avro`. `FieldStruct::Avro::Native.enabled?` tells you whether the encoder is in place. 
```ruby
require 'field_struct/avro'


defined?(FieldStruct::Avro::Native)   # => nil
```
 
```ruby
require 'field_struct/avro/native'


FieldStruct::Avro::Native.enabled?    # => true
```
 The require prepends a small module to `FieldStruct::Avro`. From then on, the calls you already make go through the plan: 
```ruby
module Signals
  class Dispatch < FieldStruct::Base
    required :id, :uuid
    required :lamp, :symbol, enum: %i[fixed flashing]
    required :swell, :big_decimal, round: 2
    required :sent, :time, zone: 'America/Denver'
    optional :channels, :array, of: :string
  end
end


dispatch = Signals::Dispatch.new(id: '3c5e7a10-92b4-4f6d-8e21-0a7d5b9c1e44', lamp: :flashing, swell: '2.75',
                                 sent: '2026-07-01T08:00:00', channels: %w[ch-16 ch-9])


bytes = FieldStruct::Avro.encode(dispatch)    # the same bytes as without the require
FieldStruct::Avro.decode(Signals::Dispatch, bytes) == dispatch   # => true
```
 Output is byte for byte what the pure path writes, for every datum the pure path accepts. `FieldStruct::Avro::Native.encode(schema, datum)` is the lower-level call. It takes a parsed `Avro::Schema` and a datum, and returns the bytes, or `nil` when it leaves the datum to Ruby. ## What falls back to pure Ruby [Section titled “What falls back to pure Ruby”](#what-falls-back-to-pure-ruby) The encoder never raises an error of its own. When it isn’t sure about a datum it returns `nil`, and the pure path then writes it or raises exactly the error the [Avro page](/adapters/avro/) describes. It falls back for: * a value of a class it doesn’t expect, such as an Integer in a `double` field or a `DateTime` in a `date` field * a value out of range: an integer past `int` or `long`, a decimal past its precision or scale, a time past `long` * a String in an encoding other than UTF-8 (7-bit US-ASCII and binary are fine) * a record datum that isn’t exactly what `to_datum` builds * a union member it can’t rule in or out the way the `avro` gem would * a schema shape it doesn’t cover: `fixed`, and logical types other than `decimal` (on bytes), `date`, `timestamp-millis`, `timestamp-micros`, `timestamp-nanos` and the identity ones like `uuid` It also copies two quirks of `avro` 1.12 by falling back. A decimal zero at scale 0 raises “Rounding necessary”, and a map with a key that isn’t valid UTF-8 raises when the map sits inside a union. A fallback costs the pure path plus the failed attempt. A schema it can’t cover is remembered, so that check isn’t repeated for each call. The plan lives on the parsed schema, so it follows the adapter’s cache. A rebuilt entry, or `FieldStruct::Avro.clear_cache`, means a new schema and a new plan. ## Install [Section titled “Install”](#install) Today `gem install field_struct-avro-native` compiles the Rust extension on your machine. You need a stable Rust toolchain and a C compiler. Precompiled gems are planned, so that installing needs no Rust. They will be built with `rb-sys` cross-compilation for `x86_64-linux`, `aarch64-linux` and `arm64-darwin`, and every other platform keeps the source gem as its fallback. The release workflow builds and smoke-tests them, but nothing has been published yet. ## Limits [Section titled “Limits”](#limits) * Only `encode` is faster. `decode`, `schema`, `to_datum` and `from_datum` are the same code as before. * Anything but an exact `String`, `Hash`, `Array`, `Time`, `Date` or `BigDecimal` falls back, subclasses included. * Ruby 3.3 or newer, and the same `avro` gem (1.12) that `field_struct-avro` uses. ## See also [Section titled “See also”](#see-also) * [Avro](/adapters/avro/) * [How adapters work](/adapters/how-adapters-work/)

# Build your own adapter

> Write a generator for a new format in about 60 lines on top of FieldStruct::Introspection, then test and package it.

Let’s write a small adapter that turns a struct into a TypeScript interface, in about 60 lines. The same steps work for any target - a docs table, a GraphQL type, a SQL definition - and the first-party adapters follow them too. ## 1. Look at what the walk gives you [Section titled “1. Look at what the walk gives you”](#1-look-at-what-the-walk-gives-you) `FieldStruct::Introspection.properties` lists a struct’s fields in order. Every property carries the external `key`, the `field` itself, and a `node` that says what kind of type it has. Here is a struct with one of each kind. 
```ruby
class Waypoint < FieldStruct::Base
  optional :label, :string
end


class Trek < FieldStruct::Base
  required :name, :string
  required :days, :integer, in: 1..30
  optional :waypoints, :array, of: Waypoint
  optional :tags, :array, of: :string
  optional :permit, :union, of: %i[integer string]
  optional :starts, :date
  serialize :json, days: 'dayCount'
end


properties = FieldStruct::Introspection.properties(Trek)
properties.map(&:key)   # => ["name", "dayCount", "waypoints", "tags", "permit", "starts"]
properties.map { |p| p.node.class.name.split('::').last }
# => ["ScalarNode", "ScalarNode", "ArrayNode", "ArrayNode", "UnionNode", "ScalarNode"]
properties[1].node.options   # => {in: 1..30}
properties[2].node.element.klass   # => Waypoint
properties[4].node.members.map { |m| m.type.class }.map { |c| c.name.split('::').last }   # => ["Integer", "String"]
```
 Nodes come in four kinds: scalar, array, union and struct. A struct node is a reference to the class and is never expanded, so nesting and recursion are yours to handle. The walk reports declarations and nothing else, which leaves what `required` means, what a missing value is and how types map up to your adapter. Every member is in the [introspection reference](/adapters/introspection/). ## 2. Write the adapter [Section titled “2. Write the adapter”](#2-write-the-adapter) The adapter is a single module function. It dispatches on the node class with `case`, since the node classes have no common base, and it matches scalar types by the node’s type class. type_script.rb 
```ruby
module TypeScript
  Types = FieldStruct::Types
  SCALARS = {
    Types::Integer => 'number', Types::Float => 'number', Types::Boolean => 'boolean',
    Types::BigDecimal => 'string', Types::Value => 'unknown'
  }.freeze


  # Returns the interface source for +klass+ and every struct it refers to.
  def self.generate(klass, serialization: :json)
    out = {}
    declare(klass, serialization, out)
    out.values.join("\n")
  end


  def self.declare(klass, serialization, out)
    name = klass.name.split('::').last
    return if out.key?(name)


    out[name] = nil # reserve the name first, so a recursive struct stops here
    lines = FieldStruct::Introspection.properties(klass, serialization: serialization).map do |prop|
      type = prop.field.options[:ts] || type_for(prop.node, serialization, out)
      optional = prop.field.required? ? '' : '?'
      "  #{prop.key}#{optional}: #{type}#{' | null' unless prop.field.required?};"
    end
    out.delete(name) # re-insert at the end, so nested interfaces come first
    out[name] = "interface #{name} {\n#{lines.join("\n")}\n}\n"
  end


  def self.type_for(node, serialization, out)
    case node
    when FieldStruct::Introspection::StructNode
      declare(node.klass, serialization, out)
      node.klass.name.split('::').last
    when FieldStruct::Introspection::ArrayNode
      "#{node.element ? type_for(node.element, serialization, out) : 'unknown'}[]"
    when FieldStruct::Introspection::UnionNode
      node.members.map { |member| type_for(member, serialization, out) }.uniq.join(' | ')
    else
      scalar(node)
    end
  end


  def self.scalar(node)
    return SCALARS.fetch(node.type.class) if SCALARS.key?(node.type.class)


    enum = node.options[:enum]
    enum ? enum.map { |value| "'#{value}'" }.join(' | ') : 'string'
  end
end
```
 A field turns into one line. An optional field gets `?` and `| null`, because `to_json` writes `null` for a missing value. A struct node declares its nested interface first and then refers to it by name. Dates, times, UUIDs and the rest of the string family fall through to `string`, which is what `to_json` writes for them. ## 3. Run it [Section titled “3. Run it”](#3-run-it) 
```ruby
TypeScript.generate(Trek) # => "interface Waypoint {\n  label?: string | null;\n}\n\ninterface Trek {\n  name: string;\n  dayCount: number;\n  waypoints?: Waypoint[] | null;\n  tags?: string[] | null;\n  permit?: number | string | null;\n  starts?: string | null;\n}\n"
```
 The key `dayCount` comes from the `serialize :json` mapping. Pass `serialization:` to get another format’s names. ## 4. Let fields override the mapping [Section titled “4. Let fields override the mapping”](#4-let-fields-override-the-mapping) The generator looks at `prop.field.options[:ts]` before anything else. Unknown options pass through a field declaration untouched, so a field can state what it wants and field_struct needs no change. 
```ruby
class Hut < FieldStruct::Base
  required :code, :string
  optional :season, :symbol, enum: %i[summer winter year_round]
  optional :places, :integer, ts: 'bigint'
end


TypeScript.generate(Hut) # => "interface Hut {\n  code: string;\n  season?: 'summer' | 'winter' | 'year_round' | null;\n  places?: bigint | null;\n}\n"
```
 A pass-through option belongs to the whole field. An array element can’t carry one, so an override covers the entire field. ## 5. Handle recursion and anonymous structs [Section titled “5. Handle recursion and anonymous structs”](#5-handle-recursion-and-anonymous-structs) A recursive struct would loop if the generator expanded nodes. Struct nodes are references, though, and the generator reserves the name before it walks the fields. 
```ruby
class Junction < FieldStruct::Base
  required :label, :string
  optional :branches, :array, of: Junction
end


TypeScript.generate(Junction) # => "interface Junction {\n  label: string;\n  branches?: Junction[] | null;\n}\n"
FieldStruct::Introspection.named?(Junction)                      # => true
FieldStruct::Introspection.named?(Class.new(FieldStruct::Base))  # => false
```
 `Introspection.named?` says whether a class can serve as a definition name. An anonymous class can’t, so a real adapter should either raise a clear error for it or inline it. ## 6. Test it [Section titled “6. Test it”](#6-test-it) Test the output as text, one struct per example, and test the claim you make about the data. Here the claim is that the interface lists exactly the keys `to_json` writes. 
```ruby
keys = Oj.load(Trek.new(name: 'Haute Route', days: 7).to_json).keys
declared = TypeScript.generate(Trek).scan(/^  (\w+)\??:/).flatten.drop(1)
declared == keys   # => true
```
 `declared` drops the first match, which is `label` from the nested `Waypoint`. In a spec that becomes `expect(declared).to eq(keys)`, with one example per type and one per override. ## 7. Package it as a gem [Section titled “7. Package it as a gem”](#7-package-it-as-a-gem) Copy the shape of the first-party adapters. field_struct-typescript.gemspec 
```ruby
Gem::Specification.new do |spec|
  spec.name = 'field_struct-typescript'
  spec.version = '1.0.0'
  spec.summary = 'TypeScript interfaces for field_struct classes'
  spec.required_ruby_version = '>= 3.3'
  spec.files = Dir['lib/**/*.rb', 'README.md', 'LICENSE.txt']
  spec.add_dependency 'field_struct', '>= 1.0.0.pre1', '< 2'
end
```
 * The entry point is `lib/field_struct/typescript.rb`, with an explicit `require_relative` per file. * Module functions come first. Add an opt-in `Methods` module only if people want `Klass.to_typescript`. * Requiring the gem must leave your users’ classes alone. * For anything you can’t map, raise `ArgumentError` naming the class and field, and don’t guess. * Depend on `field_struct` alone. Any third-party library your format needs becomes a dependency of your gem. ## See also [Section titled “See also”](#see-also) * [Introspection reference](/adapters/introspection/) * [How adapters work](/adapters/how-adapters-work/): why adapters are shaped this way * [JSON Schema](/adapters/json-schema/): a finished adapter to read

# How adapters work

> Why the adapters are separate gems on one shared walk, why they describe the JSON, and why they refuse to approximate.

This page covers why the adapters look the way they do. To use one, read that adapter’s page. To write one, go to [Build your own adapter](/adapters/build-your-own/). ## Why separate gems [Section titled “Why separate gems”](#why-separate-gems) Every adapter has its own version, changelog and release tag. Someone who wants Avro installs Avro, with no JSON Schema generator, Protobuf runtime or tracing library along for the ride, and a fix in one adapter never forces a release of another. The base gems stay small for the same reason. `field_struct` has no JSON Schema export and no `to_json_schema` method (FS-B140), and `actionable` never loads OpenTelemetry. Only the adapters that need a third-party library, like `avro` or `google-protobuf`, depend on it. The tools adapter depends on neither `mcp` nor `ruby_llm`. Each one is needed only by its own `require`, and a missing one raises an error that names the gem to add. The cost is more gems to install and keep in step. We think it is worth it, and the version ranges in each adapter’s gemspec do the keeping in step. ## Why one walk [Section titled “Why one walk”](#why-one-walk) JSON Schema, Avro and Protobuf all begin the same way: go through the fields, find each one’s external name, and see what kind of type it has. If every adapter read a struct’s internals on its own, each would lean on private details and each would hit its own bugs in the same spots. [`FieldStruct::Introspection`](/adapters/introspection/) is that first step, written once and living in `field_struct`. It reports declarations and nothing more. Nullability, defaults and type mapping differ by format, so it decides none of them. Avro writes `["null", T]` for an optional field, JSON Schema writes `"type": ["T", "null"]`, and Protobuf writes `optional`. Keeping the walk neutral lets each adapter be right about its own format. One side effect: anyone can write an adapter. Third-party exporters get the same walk as the first-party ones, and core has nothing else aimed at exporters. ## Why they describe the JSON [Section titled “Why they describe the JSON”](#why-they-describe-the-json) When a format forces a choice between Ruby behaviours, the adapters describe what a struct writes, not what it accepts. Setters are permissive - they take `'42'` for an integer and a Hash for a nested struct - while `to_json` is canonical. The JSON Schema adapter describes that canonical form (JS-I1), so the `to_json` of every valid instance validates and the schema stays short enough to read. It also explains why an optional field is nullable in JSON Schema: `to_json` writes `null` for each unset field. And a field with a default isn’t `required`, because the default fills in a missing key. ## Why never approximate [Section titled “Why never approximate”](#why-never-approximate) When an adapter can’t state a rule exactly, it raises or leaves the rule out. It never rounds, truncates or guesses. * Avro raises on a time with nanoseconds under a micros schema (AV-I1). Rounding would alter the value, and a silently altered value is the worst failure a data format can have. * Avro compares a pair of schemas before reading old bytes, because the `avro` gem would decode a decimal at the wrong scale into a wrong number without any error. * Protobuf never numbers fields by declaration order, since reordering a class would then change the wire format silently (PB-I1). It refuses a union too, because a `oneof` needs a number per member and there is no way to declare one yet. * JSON Schema omits `in:` on dates and `round:` on decimals, since it can’t express them. `pattern_for` returns `nil` unless its translation is exact (JS-I2), because a wrong pattern would make a validator in another language reject values the struct accepts. It also refuses a pattern that a backtracking JavaScript engine could take exponential time on, even though Ruby itself is safe, since those schemas get read by validators that handle untrusted input (JS-I3). Each case follows one rule: a clear error or a documented gap beats a value that is quietly wrong. The cost is that some structs can’t be exported until you make a choice, like giving every field a number. ## Why they don’t change your classes [Section titled “Why they don’t change your classes”](#why-they-dont-change-your-classes) Requiring an adapter adds a module and nothing more. After `require 'field_struct/avro'` your classes have no `to_avro` method. When you want `Hut.to_avro_schema`, include the adapter’s `Methods` module in the class. Everything also works as a module function, `FieldStruct::Avro.schema(Hut)`, with the class left alone (AV-I8). OpenTelemetry is the one exception. Tracing has to wrap `Action.run`, so requiring `actionable/otel` switches it on. It touches core in exactly two places, and results, history and exceptions come out unchanged (OT-I3, OT-I4). ## What an adapter may rely on [Section titled “What an adapter may rely on”](#what-an-adapter-may-rely-on) An adapter, whether first-party or yours, may rely on this public API, each item with a promise attached: * the `Introspection` members and the frozen shape of their nodes * the `Field` readers, the `Metadata#serialization` mapping, the type-class readers and the `ruby_type` table (FS-B155 to FS-B164) * the identity guarantees, so an adapter can cache per class and know exactly when to rebuild It may not rely on private methods, on `Types::Base` offering an export hook (core defines none, so an adapter asks with `respond_to?`), or on a pass-through option being validated. Options like `avro:` and `proto:` sit in `options` verbatim, and what they mean belongs to the adapter that reads them. ## Where we are not sure [Section titled “Where we are not sure”](#where-we-are-not-sure) The shared contract says an array of unions records only the union class, so you can’t reach the members of that union from the array node. It is documented, and the specs give no reason beyond how arrays store their element type. If you run into it, override the array field with the adapter’s own option. ## See also [Section titled “See also”](#see-also) * [Introspection reference](/adapters/introspection/) * [Build your own adapter](/adapters/build-your-own/) * [Adapters overview](/adapters/)

# Introspection reference
