```ruby
starboard = Hold.create!(name: 'starboard', cargo_tons: 0)


MoveCargo.run(source_id: port.id, target_id: starboard.id, tons: 3).success?   # => true
[port.reload.cargo_tons, starboard.reload.cargo_tons]                          # => [2, 3]


MoveCargo.run(source_id: port.id, target_id: starboard.id, tons: 9).code       # => :hold_overdrawn
[port.reload.cargo_tons, starboard.reload.cargo_tons]                          # => [2, 3]
```
 The second run pulled 9 tons from the port hold inside `lift`, and `stow` failed, so that write was undone. ## 3. Send side effects after the commit [Section titled “3. Send side effects after the commit”](#3-send-side-effects-after-the-commit) `after_commit` declares a hook that fires once the outermost transaction has committed, and only following a success. A failure, a skip or a rollback never triggers it. 
```ruby
class MoveCargo < Actionable::Action
  after_commit :announce


  private


  def announce
    HarbourMailer.cargo_shifted(input.tons).deliver_later
  end
end
```
 The run has already finished when the hook fires, so it can’t alter the returned result. If it raises, the exception propagates from the point of the commit, after the writes are saved. ## 4. Nest it in an outer transaction [Section titled “4. Nest it in an outer transaction”](#4-nest-it-in-an-outer-transaction) Inside a transaction that is already open, the action takes a savepoint of its own. A failed inner action undoes only its own writes, and the outer transaction keeps going and can still commit the rest. 
```ruby
Hold.transaction do
  MoveCargo.run(source_id: port.id, target_id: starboard.id, tons: 9).code   # => :hold_overdrawn
  # the outer transaction is still open, with the failed move undone
end
```
 To have the action join the outer transaction instead, pass `requires_new: false` to `transactional`. Either way, an `after_commit` hook waits for the outermost commit. ## See also [Section titled “See also”](#see-also) * [Rails adapter](/reference/actionable/rails/) * [Hooks and history](/reference/actionable/hooks-and-history/) * [Compose actions](/how-to/compose-actions/)

# How to run an action in a background job

> Retry a failed action by its code, discard the ones that can never succeed, and acknowledge the rest.

A `PullTimetable` job downloads a ferry timetable. A `:tide_late` answer deserves another try while `:garbled` never will, so the queue has to know which one it got. ## 1. Declare the codes and say which retry [Section titled “1. Declare the codes and say which retry”](#1-declare-the-codes-and-say-which-retry) Write the decision beside the code, as `retry:` metadata, and switch it on with `honor_retry_metadata`. The core never reads metadata by itself, so without that opt-in the job ignores it. 
```ruby
require 'actionable/job'


class PullTimetable < Actionable::Action
  honor_retry_metadata


  input { required :source, :string }


  fails :tide_late, retry: true
  fails :garbled, retry: false


  step :download


  private


  def download
    fail!(:tide_late) if input.source.include?('stalled')
    fail!(:garbled) if input.source.include?('scrambled')
    succeed('timetable loaded')
  end
end
```
 ## 2. Include the mixin in the job [Section titled “2. Include the mixin in the job”](#2-include-the-mixin-in-the-job) `run_actionable` runs the action and keeps its result. It raises `Actionable::Job::RetryableFailure` when the result deserves a retry, and for an acknowledged or discarded one it simply returns the result, so the job ends. ActiveJob needs to be told to retry that error. Sidekiq retries every job that raises, so there is nothing to add. 
```ruby
class PullTimetableJob < ApplicationJob
  include Actionable::Job::Mixin
  retry_on Actionable::Job::RetryableFailure, wait: :polynomially_longer, attempts: 5


  def perform(source)
    result = run_actionable(PullTimetable, source: source)
    Rails.logger.warn("timetable dropped: #{result.code}") if result.failure?
  end
end
```
 
```ruby
class PullTimetableWorker
  include Sidekiq::Job
  include Actionable::Job::Mixin


  def perform(source)
    run_actionable(PullTimetable, source: source)
  end
end
```
 ## 3. See each disposition [Section titled “3. See each disposition”](#3-see-each-disposition) No queue gem is needed to try the mixin. A plain object will do. 
```ruby
class TimetableRunner
  include Actionable::Job::Mixin


  def perform(source) = run_actionable(PullTimetable, source: source)
end


runner = TimetableRunner.new
runner.perform('https://example.org/quay.json').success?     # => true
runner.perform('https://example.org/scrambled').code         # => :garbled


begin
  runner.perform('https://example.org/stalled')
rescue Actionable::Job::RetryableFailure => e
  e.message      # => "actionable run failed with retryable code :tide_late"
  e.result.code  # => :tide_late
end
```
 ## 4. Change what counts as permanent [Section titled “4. Change what counts as permanent”](#4-change-what-counts-as-permanent) When `honor_retry_metadata` is off, every failure gets a retry unless its code is permanent. Out of the box the permanent codes are `:invalid_input`, `:invalid_output` and `:not_found`, because retrying input the action already refused can’t change the answer. To use another list, override `actionable_permanent_codes`. 
```ruby
class PickyRunner
  include Actionable::Job::Mixin


  def actionable_permanent_codes = %i[garbled]
end


Actionable::Job.disposition(Actionable::Failure.new(code: :tide_late))                         # => :retry
Actionable::Job.disposition(Actionable::Failure.new(code: :invalid_input))                     # => :discard
Actionable::Job.disposition(Actionable::Failure.new(code: :tide_late), permanent: [:tide_late]) # => :discard
PickyRunner.new.actionable_permanent_codes   # => [:garbled]
```
 An exception raised inside the action is not a failure. The mixin lets it through untouched, and your queue’s usual retry rules take over. ## See also [Section titled “See also”](#see-also) * [Background jobs](/reference/actionable/jobs/) * [Declared outcomes](/reference/actionable/declared-outcomes/) * [Declare and handle outcomes](/how-to/handle-action-outcomes/)

# How to use camelCase JSON keys

> Keep snake_case field names in Ruby while reading and writing camelCase keys for an outside API.

A greenhouse monitoring service sends and expects `plantedOn`, and you want `planted_on` to stay the Ruby name. ## 1. Map the names [Section titled “1. Map the names”](#1-map-the-names) Declare the fields, then call `serialize :json` with the Ruby name as key and the outside name as value. It raises on a key that isn’t a declared field, so it goes after the fields. 
```ruby
class Seedbed < FieldStruct::Base
  required :bed_label, :string
  optional :planted_on, :date, format: :iso8601
  optional :soil_type, :string


  serialize :json, bed_label: 'bedLabel', planted_on: 'plantedOn'
end
```
 Anything missing from the mapping keeps its own name, like `soil_type` here. ## 2. Write [Section titled “2. Write”](#2-write) `as_json` and `to_json` emit the outside names. Every other view of the object, `attributes`, `to_h` and pattern matching, stays with the Ruby names. 
```ruby
bed = Seedbed.new(bed_label: 'North 4', planted_on: '2026-04-14')


bed.as_json     # => {bedLabel: "North 4", plantedOn: "2026-04-14", soil_type: nil}
bed.attributes  # => {bed_label: "North 4", planted_on: Date.new(2026, 4, 14), soil_type: nil}
```
 ## 3. Read [Section titled “3. Read”](#3-read) `from_json` looks for the outside name first and falls back to the Ruby name. It ends up in `new`, so coercion and validation run as always. 
```ruby
payload = '{"bedLabel":"South 2","plantedOn":"2026-02-09","soil_type":"clay"}'
south = Seedbed.from_json(payload)


south.bed_label   # => "South 2"
south.soil_type   # => "clay"
Seedbed.from_json('{"bed_label":"East 1"}').bed_label   # => "East 1"
Seedbed.from_json(bed.to_json) == bed                   # => true
```
 A nested struct brings its own mapping, so a bed inside a greenhouse writes whatever key the bed class declares. A key that matches neither name is unknown, and `unknown_attributes` decides what happens to it. ## 4. Rename a field that clashes with a method [Section titled “4. Rename a field that clashes with a method”](#4-rename-a-field-that-clashes-with-a-method) A field can’t share a name with a method the struct already has, `errors` being one. Declare it under a different name and map it back. 
```ruby
class Alert < FieldStruct::Base
  required :level, :string
  optional :errors_field, :array, of: :string


  serialize :json, errors_field: 'errors'
end


Alert.new(level: 'frost', errors_field: ['sensor offline']).as_json   # => {level: "frost", errors: ["sensor offline"]}
```
 ## See also [Section titled “See also”](#see-also) * [JSON and serialization](/reference/field-struct/json-and-serialization/) * [Class macros](/reference/field-struct/class-macros/#serialize) * [Fields and options](/reference/field-struct/fields-and-options/)

# How to catch a fail that does not stop the run

> Enable the Actionable/VerbNotLast cop so a plain fail, succeed or skip with code after it is caught in your editor and in CI.

A step reads `fail(:deck_full) if deck.full?`, and the line below it runs regardless. Plain verbs only record an outcome and carry on, and the bang forms are the ones that stop. The cop spots the slip without executing anything. RuboCop is not in the site’s bundle, so everything on this page is plain code. ## 1. Enable the cop [Section titled “1. Enable the cop”](#1-enable-the-cop) On RuboCop 1.72 or newer, load the plugin from `.rubocop.yml`: 
```yaml
plugins:
  - actionable-rb:
      require_path: actionable/rubocop
```
 Older RuboCop versions take `require: [actionable/rubocop]` instead. Once loaded, the cop is on. ## 2. Tell it about your base classes [Section titled “2. Tell it about your base classes”](#2-tell-it-about-your-base-classes) Out of the box it treats subclasses of `Actionable::Action` and `ApplicationAction` as actions. Setting the option replaces that list, so repeat any default you still rely on. 
```yaml
Actionable/VerbNotLast:
  ActionBaseClasses:
    - Actionable::Action
    - ApplicationAction
    - Harbour::BaseAction
```
 A class in the same file counts as well, when its superclass chain reaches one of these. ## 3. Fix what it reports [Section titled “3. Fix what it reports”](#3-fix-what-it-reports) The offense lands on the call node and carries this message: 
```plaintext
`fail` records the outcome and keeps running, and more code runs after it: use `fail!` to stop here, or make it the method's last expression.
```
 Most of the time the fix is the bang form. 
```ruby
# before
def assign_berth
  fail(:deck_full) if deck.full?
  record_boarding
end


# after
def assign_berth
  fail!(:deck_full) if deck.full?
  record_boarding
end
```
 A plain verb is fine as the last statement of a method or a branch, after `return`, and on a different receiver. Bang verbs are never flagged. ## 4. Know the runtime check [Section titled “4. Know the runtime check”](#4-know-the-runtime-check) The same rule also runs without RuboCop. On the first `.run`, the action class reads its step and hook methods and raises `Actionable::DefinitionError` that names every offense, so any spec that runs the action will catch it. The cop’s job is to catch it sooner, in the editor and on each pull request. ## See also [Section titled “See also”](#see-also) * [RuboCop cop](/reference/actionable/rubocop/) * [Control flow](/reference/actionable/control-flow/#where-a-plain-verb-may-go) * [Success, failure, skip](/explanation/three-outcomes/)

# How to call one action from another

> Use an action as a step, pass values in, take outputs back, and let a child failure stop the parent.

`ClearDeparture` has to weigh the cargo before a ferry leaves, and `WeighCargo` already does that as an action of its own. ## 1. Write the child [Section titled “1. Write the child”](#1-write-the-child) The child is a normal action. It has its own input and its own output, and knows nothing about whoever calls it. 
```ruby
class WeighCargo < Actionable::Action
  input { required :manifest, :string }
  output { required :bay_label, :string }


  fails :unmanifested


  step :weigh


  private


  def weigh
    fail!(:unmanifested, 'no cargo list') unless input.manifest.end_with?('.csv', '.json')
    @bay_label = "#{input.manifest}#deck2"
  end
end
```
 ## 2. Use it as a step [Section titled “2. Use it as a step”](#2-use-it-as-a-step) Hand the class to `step`. With `input:` you list the parent’s instance variables to send, one keyword each, and `expose:` limits what comes back onto the parent after the child succeeds. 
```ruby
class ClearDeparture < Actionable::Action
  input do
    required :vessel, :string
    required :manifest, :string
  end


  output { required :bay_label, :string }


  fails :unmanifested


  step :prepare
  step WeighCargo, input: %i[manifest], expose: %i[bay_label]
  step :depart


  private


  def prepare = @manifest = input.manifest


  def depart = succeed("cleared #{input.vessel}")
end


result = ClearDeparture.run(vessel: 'Gannet', manifest: 'tuesday.csv')
result.message     # => "cleared Gannet"
result.bay_label   # => "tuesday.csv#deck2"
ClearDeparture.steps.map(&:kind)   # => [:method, :action, :method]
```
 Values cross over as instance variables, which is why `prepare` sets `@manifest`. An unset variable goes out as `nil`, and a field the child leaves unset never overwrites one the parent already holds. ## 3. Let a child failure stop the parent [Section titled “3. Let a child failure stop the parent”](#3-let-a-child-failure-stop-the-parent) When the child fails, the parent fails the same way: same code, message, errors and metadata, and none of the later steps run. The parent must declare every code its children can fail with. If it doesn’t, the run raises `DefinitionError`. 
```ruby
failed = ClearDeparture.run(vessel: 'Gannet', manifest: 'tuesday.txt')
failed.failure?   # => true
failed.code       # => :unmanifested
failed.message    # => "no cargo list"
```
 ## 4. Remember that a child skip does not stop anything [Section titled “4. Remember that a child skip does not stop anything”](#4-remember-that-a-child-skip-does-not-stop-anything) The parent does not see a skip as an outcome. It carries on as though the child had succeeded. To stop on a skip, check for it in the step that follows. ## 5. Look at what ran [Section titled “5. Look at what ran”](#5-look-at-what-ran) History is off until you ask. `measure :all` records each step that ran, and when the parent measures, the children measure too. 
```ruby
class Logged < ClearDeparture
  measure :all
end


history = Logged.run(vessel: 'Gannet', manifest: 'tuesday.csv').history
history.steps.map(&:name)             # => [:prepare, WeighCargo, :depart]
history.steps[1].nested.steps.map(&:name)   # => [:weigh]
```
 ## See also [Section titled “See also”](#see-also) * [Actions and steps](/reference/actionable/actions-and-steps/#nested-actions) * [Hooks and history](/reference/actionable/hooks-and-history/) * [Composing actions](/tutorial/composing-actions/)

# How to handle dates, times and time zones

> Parse dates and timestamps from strings, demand an offset where it matters, and read local wall-clock times in a named zone.

A `Planting` takes a date and a watering time from a form, and every machine has to read them the same way. ## 1. Pick the format [Section titled “1. Pick the format”](#1-pick-the-format) With no `format:`, a date or time field reads a String through the standard library, and that is lenient. When you know what the input looks like, declare it. A preset such as `:us` or `:eu` is shorthand for a `strptime` String, and a String is used as given. 
```ruby
class Planting < FieldStruct::Base
  required :crop, :string
  required :day, :date, format: :eu
  optional :watering, :time, format: '%H:%M', zone: 'UTC'
end


planting = Planting.new(crop: 'Tulips', day: '14/04/2026')
planting.day.iso8601                                      # => "2026-04-14"
Planting.new(crop: 'Tulips', day: '14/04/2026 ').valid?   # => false
Planting.new(crop: 'Tulips', day: '31/04/2026').valid?    # => false
```
 A declared format must match the whole String, so a trailing space fails, and the day must exist on the calendar. `as_json` writes with the same format, which means a JSON round trip keeps it. ## 2. Require an offset with `:iso8601` [Section titled “2. Require an offset with :iso8601”](#2-require-an-offset-with-iso8601) For timestamps that travel between systems, use `format: :iso8601`. That means RFC 3339, so a String without an offset is refused, not guessed at. 
```ruby
class Sample < FieldStruct::Base
  required :rain_mm, :float
  required :taken_at, :time, format: :iso8601
end


Sample.new(rain_mm: '4.5', taken_at: '2026-04-14T06:15:00-06:00').taken_at.utc_offset  # => -21600
Sample.new(rain_mm: '4.5', taken_at: '2026-04-14T06:15:00').valid?                     # => false
Sample.new(rain_mm: '4.5', taken_at: '2026-04-14 06:15:00Z').valid?                    # => false
```
 Caution A `Z` produces a fixed `+00:00` offset, not UTC. The instant is correct and `==` against a UTC time holds, but `utc?` is false. Call `.utc` on the value when you need it. ## 3. Say which zone a local time means [Section titled “3. Say which zone a local time means”](#3-say-which-zone-a-local-time-means) A wall-clock String with no offset is ambiguous. Without `zone:` it lands in whatever zone the process happens to run in. Declare the zone and the gem applies the offset that was in force on that date. 
```ruby
class Watering < FieldStruct::Base
  required :starts, :time, zone: 'America/Chicago'
end


Watering.new(starts: '2026-07-01 10:00').starts.utc_offset          # => -18000
Watering.new(starts: '2026-01-15 10:00').starts.utc_offset          # => -21600
Watering.new(starts: '2026-07-01 10:00 +02:00').starts.utc_offset   # => 7200
```
 `zone:` accepts `"UTC"`, a fixed offset like `"-06:00"` (the colon is required) or an IANA name with a slash. An abbreviation such as `"CST"` raises when the class is defined, which beats silently meaning something else. A String or object that carries its own offset keeps it. ## 4. Know the daylight-saving edges [Section titled “4. Know the daylight-saving edges”](#4-know-the-daylight-saving-edges) With an IANA zone, a time the clock skips is refused. A time the clock repeats is read at the earlier, daylight offset. 
```ruby
Watering.new(starts: '2026-03-08 02:30').valid?               # => false
Watering.new(starts: '2026-11-01 01:30').starts.utc_offset    # => -18000
```
 What you get back is a plain `Time` with the resolved offset. It doesn’t remember the zone, so adding days to it won’t follow daylight saving. If you need a fresh reading, assign the field again from the String. ## 5. Pin your examples and tests [Section titled “5. Pin your examples and tests”](#5-pin-your-examples-and-tests) In tests, use a fixed timestamp and a declared `zone:`. A format that covers only part of a day, like `'%H:%M'` above, fills in today’s date, so assert on `hour` and `min` rather than on the whole value. 
```ruby
Planting.new(crop: 'Tulips', day: '14/04/2026', watering: '07:45').watering.hour   # => 7
```
 ## See also [Section titled “See also”](#see-also) * [Dates, times and zones](/reference/field-struct/dates-and-times/) * [JSON and serialization](/reference/field-struct/json-and-serialization/) * [Coercion versus validation](/explanation/coercion-and-validation/)

# How to generate RBS signatures for your classes

> Write RBS for your structs and actions so Steep or Solargraph can type-check the code that uses them.

Structs and actions are built from macros, so a type checker can’t see their fields. Generate the signatures and skip writing them by hand. ## 1. Generate for a struct [Section titled “1. Generate for a struct”](#1-generate-for-a-struct) `FieldStruct::RBS.generate` takes a named struct class and returns RBS source with a reader and a writer for each field. 
```ruby
class Fern < FieldStruct::Base
  required :species, :string
  optional :fronds, :integer
  optional :habitats, :array, of: :string
end


fern_rbs = FieldStruct::RBS.generate(Fern)
fern_rbs.include?('attr_reader species: ::String')                # => true
fern_rbs.include?('attr_reader fronds: ::Integer?')               # => true
fern_rbs.include?('def species=: (::String? value) -> ::String?') # => true
```
 ## 2. Generate for an action [Section titled “2. Generate for an action”](#2-generate-for-an-action) `Actionable::RBS.generate` writes the class with a typed `run`, plus input, output and result classes and aliases for the codes. The class must be named. 
```ruby
class ReleaseBerth < Actionable::Action
  input do
    required :voyage_id, :integer
    optional :remark, :string
  end
  output { required :berths_freed, :integer }


  fails :deck_full
  skips :already_ashore
end


action_rbs = Actionable::RBS.generate(ReleaseBerth)
action_rbs.lines.first.chomp   # => "class ReleaseBerth < ::Actionable::Action"
action_rbs.include?('def self.run: (voyage_id: ::Integer, ?remark: ::String?) -> Result')   # => true
action_rbs.include?('type failure_code = :deck_full | :invalid_input | :invalid_output')     # => true
```
 ## 3. Write the files [Section titled “3. Write the files”](#3-write-the-files) Put both generators in one rake task and commit what they write beside your code. A changed field then shows up in review. lib/tasks/rbs.rake 
```ruby
task :rbs do
  { Fern => 'fern', ReleaseBerth => 'release_berth' }.each do |klass, file|
    generator = klass < Actionable::Action ? Actionable::RBS : FieldStruct::RBS
    File.write(File.join('sig', "#{file}.rbs"), generator.generate(klass))
  end
end
```
 ## 4. Remember what the types promise [Section titled “4. Remember what the types promise”](#4-remember-what-the-types-promise) Caution The types describe a valid instance. An invalid struct may hold something else, because the default `:keep_raw` policy keeps the raw input. Check `valid?` before trusting a type. Required readers carry no `?`, while writers always accept `nil`. A free-form action (no `input` block, or one that dispatches through `input_for`) gets a `run` taking `(*untyped)`. Generation raises `ArgumentError` for an anonymous class, or when Ruby already resolves `InputSchema` on it, and the message says what to rename. ## See also [Section titled “See also”](#see-also) * [Introspection and tooling](/reference/field-struct/introspection-and-tooling/#rbs) * [RBS for your actions](/reference/actionable/rbs/) * [Declare and handle outcomes](/how-to/handle-action-outcomes/)

# How to declare failures and handle each outcome

> Name the ways an action can end, attach messages and metadata, and branch on each outcome at the call site.

An `IssuePass` action has four possible endings, and whoever calls it should tell them apart by code, not by reading text. ## 1. Declare the failures and skips [Section titled “1. Declare the failures and skips”](#1-declare-the-failures-and-skips) `fails` and `skips` name the codes the action may end with. A message and metadata are optional. When a call gives no message, the declared one is used. 
```ruby
class IssuePass < Actionable::Action
  input do
    optional :callsign, :string
    optional :boarded, :boolean, default: false
  end


  fails :missing_callsign, 'a pass needs a callsign'
  skips :already_boarded


  step :check


  private


  def check
    skip!(:already_boarded, 'already aboard') if input.boarded
    fail!(:missing_callsign) if input.callsign.nil?
    succeed('pass issued')
  end
end
```
 ## 2. Know that declaring makes it strict [Section titled “2. Know that declaring makes it strict”](#2-know-that-declaring-makes-it-strict) After an action declares one failure code, it can fail only with declared ones. The gem’s own `:invalid_input` and `:invalid_output` stay allowed. Skips work the same way, and declaring one kind never makes the other strict. 
```ruby
class Misspelt < Actionable::Action
  fails :missing_callsign
  step :check


  private


  def check = fail!(:missing_callsgin)
end


begin
  Misspelt.run
rescue Actionable::DefinitionError => e
  e.message.start_with?('Misspelt fails with :missing_callsgin, which it does not declare')   # => true
end
```
 So a typo in a code turns into a loud error on the first run, and not into a result nobody handles. ## 3. Read the code [Section titled “3. Read the code”](#3-read-the-code) Each result carries a `status` (`:success`, `:failure` or `:skipped`) and a `code` with the specific reason. A skip is not a failure, so `ok?` is true for it. 
```ruby
IssuePass.run(callsign: 'GANNET').status                       # => :success
IssuePass.run.code                                             # => :missing_callsign
IssuePass.run.message                                          # => "a pass needs a callsign"
IssuePass.run(callsign: 'GANNET', boarded: true).skipped?      # => true
IssuePass.run(callsign: 'GANNET', boarded: true).ok?           # => true
IssuePass.run(boarded: 'maybe').code                           # => :invalid_input
```
 ## 4. Branch at the call site [Section titled “4. Branch at the call site”](#4-branch-at-the-call-site) Results work with `case/in`. The class tells you which outcome it is, and the code tells you why. 
```ruby
def describe_pass(**params)
  case IssuePass.run(**params)
  in Actionable::Success                             then 'aboard'
  in Actionable::Skipped[code: :already_boarded]     then 'nothing to do'
  in Actionable::Failure[code: :missing_callsign]    then 'add a callsign'
  in Actionable::Failure[code: :invalid_input]       then 'bad request'
  end
end


describe_pass(callsign: 'GANNET')                      # => "aboard"
describe_pass(callsign: 'GANNET', boarded: true)       # => "nothing to do"
describe_pass                                          # => "add a callsign"
describe_pass(boarded: 'maybe')                        # => "bad request"
```
 Add an `else` if more codes may show up later. Without it, a result that no branch matches raises `NoMatchingPatternError`. In a controller that is usually what you want, since failing loudly beats ignoring it. ## 5. Read the declarations [Section titled “5. Read the declarations”](#5-read-the-declarations) `declared_failures` and `declared_skips` hand a program the same list a person would read. 
```ruby
IssuePass.declared_failures   # => {missing_callsign: {}}
IssuePass.declared_skips      # => {already_boarded: {}}
```
 The [background job adapter](/how-to/background-jobs/) and the test helpers both read that list. Keep it honest, and add a code before you start using it. ## See also [Section titled “See also”](#see-also) * [Declared outcomes](/reference/actionable/declared-outcomes/) * [Control flow](/reference/actionable/control-flow/) * [Declared failures and result codes](/explanation/declared-outcomes/) * [Compose actions](/how-to/compose-actions/)

# How to keep sensitive values out of logs

> Mark passwords and tokens as sensitive so inspect, pp and loggers print a placeholder, and filter the data paths yourself.

A `Gardener` record carries a password and an API token, and sooner or later someone will `inspect` it, `pp` it or pass it to a logger. ## 1. Mark the fields [Section titled “1. Mark the fields”](#1-mark-the-fields) `sensitive: true` is accepted on every type. 
```ruby
require 'logger'
require 'stringio'


class Gardener < FieldStruct::Base
  required :email, :email
  optional :password, :string, sensitive: true
  optional :api_token, :string, sensitive: true
  optional :allotments, :integer
end


gardener = Gardener.new(email: 'rows@example.org', password: 'compost9', api_token: 'sprout_key_5521')
```
 ## 2. Check what prints [Section titled “2. Check what prints”](#2-check-what-prints) `inspect` prints `[FILTERED]` for a value that is set and `nil` for one that isn’t, so an unset secret still looks different from a set one. 
```ruby
gardener.inspect  # => '#<Gardener email: "rows@example.org", password: [FILTERED], api_token: [FILTERED], allotments: nil>'
Gardener.new(email: 'rows@example.org').inspect.include?('password: nil')   # => true
```
 Loggers call `inspect` on what they receive, and `pp` shares the same masking, nested structs and their arrays included. 
```ruby
io = StringIO.new
Logger.new(io).info(gardener)
io.string.include?('sprout_key_5521')   # => false
io.string.include?('[FILTERED]')        # => true
```
 ## 3. Mind the data paths [Section titled “3. Mind the data paths”](#3-mind-the-data-paths) The mask is for display only (FS-I15). Readers, `attributes`, `to_h`, `as_json` and `to_json` return the real value, since code that serializes an object has to have it. 
```ruby
gardener.password          # => "compost9"
gardener.to_h[:api_token]  # => "sprout_key_5521"
```
 Caution `puts gardener.to_h` and `logger.info(gardener.to_json)` both print the secrets. The mask covers `inspect` and `pp`, and nothing more. ## 4. Filter before you log or send a hash [Section titled “4. Filter before you log or send a hash”](#4-filter-before-you-log-or-send-a-hash) The field metadata knows which fields are sensitive. Filter with it instead of keeping a list by hand. 
```ruby
safe = gardener.to_h.reject { |name, _| Gardener.metadata[name].sensitive? }
safe.keys   # => [:email, :allotments]
```
 Error messages never contain the value, only its class, so the `errors:` part of `inspect` is safe as well. ## See also [Section titled “See also”](#see-also) * [Introspection and tooling](/reference/field-struct/introspection-and-tooling/#sensitive-fields) * [Instance API](/reference/field-struct/instance-api/) * [Fields and options](/reference/field-struct/fields-and-options/)

# How to validate nested structs and arrays

> Declare nested shapes, find the element that is wrong, and re-assign after changing a child in place.

A `Planting` holds several seedlings, one has a leaf count that is out of range, and you need to say which. ## 1. Declare the shape [Section titled “1. Declare the shape”](#1-declare-the-shape) A struct class works as a type, so a field can hold one, and `:array` with `of:` holds a list. Hashes passed in for them are built into structs. 
```ruby
class Seedling < FieldStruct::Base
  required :species, :string
  required :leaves, :integer, in: 1..99
end


class Planting < FieldStruct::Base
  required :label, :string
  required :seedlings, :array, of: Seedling
  optional :showpiece, Seedling
end


planting = Planting.new(label: 'P7', seedlings: [{ species: 'fern', leaves: 12 }, { species: 'moss', leaves: 0 }])
```
 ## 2. Read the parent’s verdict [Section titled “2. Read the parent’s verdict”](#2-read-the-parents-verdict) One invalid child makes the parent invalid. The parent says so once, on the field, and doesn’t name the element. 
```ruby
planting.valid?                    # => false
planting.errors.to_h               # => {seedlings: ["is invalid"]}
planting.seedlings.first.valid?    # => true
```
 ## 3. Find the element [Section titled “3. Find the element”](#3-find-the-element) Every element is a struct with its own errors, so ask each one. If you want an indexed report, build it yourself. 
```ruby
bad = planting.seedlings.each_with_index.reject { |seedling, _| seedling.valid? }
bad.map(&:last)                    # => [1]


report = planting.seedlings.each_with_index.to_h { |s, i| [i, s.errors.to_h] }.reject { |_, errors| errors.empty? }
report                             # => {1 => {leaves: ["is invalid"]}}
```
 The `"element at index 1 is invalid"` message belongs to plain elements checked through `element:` options, like strings with a `format:`. A struct element reports through itself instead. ## 4. Re-assign after changing a child in place [Section titled “4. Re-assign after changing a child in place”](#4-re-assign-after-changing-a-child-in-place) Validity gets computed when a value is assigned (see [Validity is computed on assignment](/explanation/validity-on-assignment/)). If you change a child later, the parent doesn’t notice, whichever way the change goes. 
```ruby
planting.seedlings[1].leaves = 3
planting.valid?                    # => false, still stale


planting.seedlings = planting.seedlings
planting.valid?                    # => true
```
 Assigning the field again runs coercion and validation again. `with` does too, and it hands back a new object: 
```ruby
Planting.new(label: 'P7', seedlings: [{ species: 'fern', leaves: 12 }]).with(label: 'P8').label   # => "P8"
```
 Caution Pushing onto the array in place, `planting.seedlings << { species: 'x' }`, stores the raw element with no coercion. Build a fresh array and assign that. ## See also [Section titled “See also”](#see-also) * [Types](/reference/field-struct/types/) * [Validation and errors](/reference/field-struct/validation-and-errors/#validity-is-computed-on-assignment) * [Validity is computed on assignment](/explanation/validity-on-assignment/)

# How to test structs and actions with RSpec

> Assert an action's outcome in one line, build results without stubbing, and replace a collaborator action.

Say you have a `Voyage` struct and a `LogCrossing` action, and you want specs that read like the outcome they check. `actionable/rspec` needs RSpec, and the site’s bundle has none. So most of this page is plain code. The matcher and the result builders are tried on a small object that extends the same modules. ## 1. Load the helpers [Section titled “1. Load the helpers”](#1-load-the-helpers) spec/spec_helper.rb 
```ruby
require 'actionable/rspec'
```
 If `RSpec` is defined, this pulls the matcher and the stub helpers into every example group. Coverage is a separate require, `actionable/rspec/coverage`. ## 2. Test a struct [Section titled “2. Test a struct”](#2-test-a-struct) A struct spec is ordinary RSpec. Check validity and the messages, and leave `inspect` alone. 
```ruby
RSpec.describe Voyage do
  it 'needs a route' do
    voyage = described_class.new(route: '')


    expect(voyage).not_to be_valid
    expect(voyage.errors[:route]).to eq(['is required'])
  end


  it 'reads a date from a string' do
    expect(described_class.new(route: 'Quay-Isle', departs_on: '2026-07-03').departs_on).to eq(Date.new(2026, 7, 3))
  end
end
```
 ## 3. Test the outcome in one line [Section titled “3. Test the outcome in one line”](#3-test-the-outcome-in-one-line) `perform_actionable` calls `.run` on the class and inspects the result. On its own it expects success, and the chains name every other outcome. 
```ruby
RSpec.describe LogCrossing do
  it { is_expected.to perform_actionable(route: 'Quay-Isle') }
  it { is_expected.to perform_actionable(route: 'Quay-Isle', logged: true).and_skip(:already_logged) }
  it { is_expected.to perform_actionable.and_fail(:missing_route, /needs a route/) }
end
```
 If the action declares its codes, asking for one it doesn’t declare raises `ArgumentError` inside the spec. That is on purpose, because such a spec would assert an outcome the action can never produce. To show it, we ran the matcher outside RSpec: 
```ruby
require 'actionable/rspec'


class LogCrossing < Actionable::Action
  input { optional :route, :string }


  fails :missing_route, 'a crossing needs a route'
  skips :already_logged


  step :check


  private


  def check
    fail!(:missing_route) if input.route.nil?
    succeed
  end
end


spec = Object.new.extend(Actionable::RSpec::Matchers, Actionable::RSpec::Stubs)


spec.perform_actionable(route: 'Quay-Isle').matches?(LogCrossing)                                # => true
spec.perform_actionable.and_fail(:missing_route, /needs a route/).matches?(LogCrossing)          # => true
spec.perform_actionable.and_skip(:already_logged).matches?(LogCrossing)                          # => false


begin
  spec.perform_actionable.and_fail(:missing_rout).matches?(LogCrossing)
rescue ArgumentError => e
  e.message.start_with?('LogCrossing does not declare :missing_rout')   # => true
end
```
 ## 4. Build a result without running anything [Section titled “4. Build a result without running anything”](#4-build-a-result-without-running-anything) The builders return a real result, handy when you test code that only consumes one. 
```ruby
failure = spec.actionable_failure_result(code: :missing_route, errors: { route: 'is required' })
failure.code         # => :missing_route
failure.errors.to_h  # => {route: ["is required"]}
spec.actionable_skip_result(code: :already_logged).skipped?   # => true
spec.actionable_success_result(output: { leg: 4 }).output     # => {leg: 4}
```
 ## 5. Stub a collaborator action [Section titled “5. Stub a collaborator action”](#5-stub-a-collaborator-action) When one action calls another, swap the collaborator out with `allow_actionable_failure`, `allow_actionable_success` or `allow_actionable_skip`. Each builds a real result and stubs `run` through rspec-mocks. The `stub_actionable_*` forms also expect the call to happen. 
```ruby
RSpec.describe Embark do
  it 'stops when the deck is full' do
    allow_actionable_failure(ReserveBerth, code: :deck_full)


    expect(Embark).to perform_actionable(callsign: 'x').and_fail(:deck_full)
  end
end
```
 A stubbed code that the collaborator doesn’t declare raises, exactly like the matcher. And when the collaborator has an output schema, the stubbed output goes through it. ## See also [Section titled “See also”](#see-also) * [Testing](/reference/actionable/testing/) * [Declare and handle outcomes](/how-to/handle-action-outcomes/) * [Compose actions](/how-to/compose-actions/)

# How to validate request params

> Turn untrusted string-keyed params into a typed struct, report what is wrong as JSON, and do the same through an action's input.

A form or an API call hands you a Hash of params. Every key is a String, and every value is a String or a list of them. You want either a typed object or a list of what is wrong. ## 1. Declare the shape [Section titled “1. Declare the shape”](#1-declare-the-shape) List the fields you accept. String keys work as they are, and values are coerced when assigned (see [Types](/reference/field-struct/types/)). 
```ruby
class NewCrossing < FieldStruct::Base
  unknown_attributes :raise


  required :route, :string
  optional :stops, :array, of: :string
  optional :departs_on, :date, format: :iso8601
end


params = { 'route' => 'Quay to Isle', 'stops' => %w[Pier Skerry], 'departs_on' => '2026-07-03' }
crossing = NewCrossing.new(params)


crossing.valid?              # => true
crossing.departs_on.to_s     # => "2026-07-03"
crossing.stops               # => ["Pier", "Skerry"]
```
 `unknown_attributes :raise` is optional. We lean on it for params: a key nobody expected is usually a typo, or someone poking around. With the default, `:ignore`, it vanishes quietly. ## 2. Rescue the keys you did not expect [Section titled “2. Rescue the keys you did not expect”](#2-rescue-the-keys-you-did-not-expect) Under `:raise` the whole call fails before any value is assigned, and the error names every unknown key. 
```ruby
begin
  NewCrossing.new('route' => 'Quay to Isle', 'pilot' => 'true', 'flag' => 'x')
rescue FieldStruct::UnknownAttributeError => e
  e.keys      # => [:pilot, :flag]
  e.message   # => "unknown attribute(s) for NewCrossing: :pilot, :flag"
end
```
 ## 3. Report the errors [Section titled “3. Report the errors”](#3-report-the-errors) A bad value never makes `new` raise. You get an object with `errors`, and the messages never include the input, which makes them safe to log and to send back (FS-I5). 
```ruby
bad = NewCrossing.new('route' => '', 'departs_on' => '2026-02-30')


bad.valid?                        # => false
bad.errors.to_h.keys              # => [:route, :departs_on]
bad.errors[:route]                # => ["is required"]
bad.errors.full_messages.first    # => "Route is required"
Oj.dump(bad.errors.to_h, mode: :compat).start_with?('{"route":["is required"]')  # => true
```
 Use `errors.to_h` as the body of a 422, and keep `full_messages` for flash text. Check `valid?` before trusting any value, because with the default `:keep_raw` policy an invalid field may still hold what was sent. ## 4. Do the same through an action [Section titled “4. Do the same through an action”](#4-do-the-same-through-an-action) An action’s `input` block is the same FieldStruct body. Splat the params so each key turns into a keyword, and a bad value ends the run as `:invalid_input` before a step gets to run. 
```ruby
class ScheduleCrossing < Actionable::Action
  input do
    required :route, :string
    optional :stops, :array, of: :string
    optional :departs_on, :date, format: :iso8601
  end


  step :schedule


  private


  def schedule = succeed("scheduled #{input.route}")
end


ScheduleCrossing.run(**params).message          # => "scheduled Quay to Isle"


result = ScheduleCrossing.run(**{ 'route' => '' })
result.code                                     # => :invalid_input
result.errors.to_h                              # => {route: ["is required"]}
```
 Caution Write `**params`, never `params`. A positional Hash is ignored without a word, and the run ends as `:invalid_input` with “is required” on every required field. See [Input and output](/reference/actionable/input-and-output/). ## See also [Section titled “See also”](#see-also) * [Validation and errors](/reference/field-struct/validation-and-errors/) * [Class macros](/reference/field-struct/class-macros/#unknown_attributes) * [Input and output](/reference/actionable/input-and-output/) * [Nested structs and arrays](/how-to/nested-structs-and-arrays/)

Come here to look something up. A page takes one topic and covers all of it: options in a table, an example CI runs for each behaviour, and for each error what raises it. Nothing here teaches. New to the gems? The [tutorial](/tutorial/trek-planner/) comes first, and the [explanation](/explanation/) pages give the reasons behind the design. ## Versions covered [Section titled “Versions covered”](#versions-covered) These pages describe field_struct 1.0.0.pre1 and actionable-rb 2.0.0.pre1. The block below is a checked example too, so the build fails if the versions named here drift from the code. versions.rb 
```ruby

```
 If a page and the code disagree, the code wins and the page has a bug. # Actions and steps

> How to declare an action, run it, and build it from method steps, case steps and nested actions.

An action subclasses `Actionable::Action`, and each of its steps is an instance method (public or private). Every `.run` creates a new instance, so nothing carries over between calls. Here we cover how to declare steps and how to run them. The verbs that end a run live on [Control flow](/reference/actionable/control-flow/), and the object `.run` hands back is on [Results](/reference/actionable/results/). ## `.run` [Section titled “.run”](#run) 
```ruby
Action.run(*args, **kwargs) # => Result
```
 When the class has an `input` block, `.run(**kwargs)` builds the input struct and the steps read it as `input` (see [Input and output](/reference/actionable/input-and-output/)). Without the block the action is free-form, so `.run` forwards `*args` and `**kwargs` to `new` and the default constructor discards them. You always get exactly one result back, and a run that records no outcome succeeds on its own (AC §5.4). 
```ruby
class StampPass < Actionable::Action
  input { required :pass_no, :string }
  output { required :stamped_no, :string }


  step :stamp


  private


  def stamp
    @stamped_no = input.pass_no.delete('/')
  end
end


StampPass.run(pass_no: 'BP/204/7').stamped_no # => "BP2047"
StampPass.steps.map(&:name)                   # => [:stamp]
StampPass.run.code                            # => :invalid_input
```
 A free-form action that wants its own arguments overrides `initialize` and calls `super()`: 
```ruby
class Hail < Actionable::Action
  def initialize(vessel)
    super()
    @vessel = vessel
  end


  step :hail


  private


  def hail = succeed("ahoy #{@vessel}")
end


Hail.run('Gannet').message        # => "ahoy Gannet"
Actionable::Action.run.success?   # => true
```
 Caution When an action has an `input` block, the gem calls `new` with no arguments and assigns the input afterwards. An `initialize` override has to work with zero required arguments and call `super`, or `.run` raises Ruby’s `ArgumentError` (AC §5.6). ## `step` [Section titled “step”](#step) 
```ruby
step(target, **options)
```
 | Target | Kind | Meaning | | -------------------- | --------- | ----------------------------------------------------------------- | | Symbol or String | `:method` | call the instance method of that name | | an `Action` subclass | `:action` | run that action as a step (see [nested actions](#nested-actions)) | | Option | Applies to | Meaning | | --------- | ------------ | ------------------------------------------------ | | `if:` | all steps | run only when the guard is truthy | | `unless:` | all steps | skip when the guard is truthy | | `input:` | action steps | names of instance variables to pass to the child | | `expose:` | action steps | narrow the child’s output fields copied back | Steps execute in the order declared, and `step 'name'` is the same step as `step :name`. Any other kind of target (`step 5`) raises `ArgumentError` with `unsupported step target`. A step object answers `name`, `kind` and `options`, and two steps are equal when their class and name match. `Action.steps` lists them in order (AC §5.3). ### Guards [Section titled “Guards”](#guards) `if:` and `unless:` accept a method name or a callable that gets the instance. If both are present, whichever one asks to skip wins. A step that is skipped this way never runs and leaves no entry in the history. Guard methods can be private, but the missing-method check ignores them, so a misspelled guard raises `NoMethodError` only when it is evaluated, partway through a run (AC-B#guards). 
```ruby
class ClearDeparture < Actionable::Action
  input { required :vessel, :string }


  step :inspect_hull
  step :scrub_ballast, if: :ballast_dirty?
  step :ring_bell, unless: ->(action) { action.input.vessel.length < 5 }
  step :cast_off


  def initialize
    super
    @trail = []
  end


  private


  def ballast_dirty? = false
  def inspect_hull = @trail << :inspect_hull
  def scrub_ballast = @trail << :scrub_ballast
  def ring_bell = @trail << :ring_bell
  def cast_off = succeed(@trail.join(','))
end


ClearDeparture.run(vessel: 'Heron II').message  # => "inspect_hull,ring_bell"
ClearDeparture.run(vessel: 'Tern').message      # => "inspect_hull"
ClearDeparture.steps.to_a[1].options            # => {if: :ballast_dirty?}
```
 ### Redeclaration and inheritance [Section titled “Redeclaration and inheritance”](#redeclaration-and-inheritance) Declare a step a second time (same kind, same name) and its options are replaced where it stands, so it keeps its first position. `step :x` and `case_step :x` count as different steps, and so do `on_success :x` and `always :x`, since every hook list is separate. A subclass takes a copy of the steps, hooks, schemas, declarations, `measure` and `typed_outcomes` at creation time (AC-I6). The parent can change afterwards without affecting it, and redeclaring a step in the subclass touches the subclass only. An inherited step can’t be removed, so switch it off with `if:` or `unless:`. 
```ruby
class BaseDeparture < Actionable::Action
  step :load_deck
  step :sound_horn


  private


  def load_deck = nil
  def sound_horn = nil
end


class SilentDeparture < BaseDeparture
  step :sound_horn, unless: ->(_action) { true }
end


BaseDeparture.steps.map(&:options)  # => [{}, {}]
SilentDeparture.steps.map(&:name)   # => [:load_deck, :sound_horn]
SilentDeparture.steps.to_a.last.options.keys # => [:unless]
SilentDeparture.steps.equal?(BaseDeparture.steps) # => false
```
 ## `case_step` [Section titled “case_step”](#case_step) 
```ruby
case_step(value_method, **options) { on(match, target, **opts); default(target, **opts) }
```
 A case step calls the named method (private works), then runs the first branch whose match accepts the returned value. The target is either a method name or an action class, and `default` takes whatever is left. | Match | Accepts | | -------- | ---------------------------- | | a value | values equal to it (`==`) | | an Array | any value the Array includes | | a Regexp | values whose `to_s` matches | `Actionable::ValueMatch.matches?(branch_value, value)` is the matcher everything shares. A Range never matches one of its members since it is compared with `==` only, so spell it `(400..499).to_a` rather than `400..499`. A `nil` branch matches `nil` (AC-B#valuematch). 
```ruby
class AssignQuay < Actionable::Action
  input { required :cargo, :string }


  case_step :cargo do
    on 'foot', :use_gangway
    on %w[car van], :use_ramp
    on(/\Acrane/, :use_hoist)
    default :use_tender
  end


  private


  def cargo = input.cargo
  def use_gangway = succeed('gangway')
  def use_ramp = succeed('ramp')
  def use_hoist = succeed('hoist')
  def use_tender = succeed('tender')
end


AssignQuay.run(cargo: 'van').message        # => "ramp"
AssignQuay.run(cargo: 'crane-lift').message # => "hoist"
AssignQuay.run(cargo: 'livestock').message  # => "tender"
AssignQuay.steps.first.kind                 # => :case
Actionable::ValueMatch.matches?((400..499), 450) # => false
Actionable::ValueMatch.matches?((400..499).to_a, 450) # => true
```
 When nothing matches and there is no `default`, the step does nothing and no error is raised. Branches accept `if:` and `unless:` just like steps do. The value alone picks the branch, and only then does its guard decide whether the target runs. If the matched branch’s guard opts out, nothing happens and a later branch is not tried. `case_step` is built on `Steps::Case.define(value_source, **options)`, and the resulting step answers `branches` (`[match_value, step]` pairs) and `default_step`. ## Nested actions [Section titled “Nested actions”](#nested-actions) 
```ruby
step ChildAction, input: %i[berth], expose: %i[pass]
```
 A nested action amounts to `Child.run(berth: @berth)`. For each name listed in `input:`, the parent hands over its instance variable of that name as a keyword, and an unset variable goes in as `nil`. After a success or a skip, the fields the child really set come back to the parent as instance variables, limited by `expose:`. A field the child never set can’t overwrite what the parent holds (AC-B#nestedabsorb). 
```ruby
class HoldBerth < Actionable::Action
  input { required :berth, :string }
  output { required :held, :boolean }


  step :hold


  private


  def hold = @held = true
end


class IssueBoarding < Actionable::Action
  step HoldBerth, input: %i[berth], expose: %i[held]
  step :finish


  def initialize
    super
    @berth = 'D2-17'
    @deck = 'upper'
  end


  private


  def finish = succeed("held=#{@held} deck=#{@deck}")
end


IssueBoarding.run.message # => "held=true deck=upper"
IssueBoarding.steps.first.kind # => :action
```
 When the child fails, the parent fails too and stops. It takes over the child’s code, message, errors and metadata directly, never passing through its own `fail`. A parent that declares any failure code has to declare every non-gem code its children can fail with, otherwise `DefinitionError` is raised, while the gem’s own `:invalid_input` and `:invalid_output` pass through freely (AC §5.8, AC-B#nesteddeclare). A child’s skip doesn’t count as an outcome for the parent, which carries on as it would after a success. If the parent measures, the child’s history is attached to the matching step record (see [Hooks and history](/reference/actionable/hooks-and-history/)). ## Checks before and during a run [Section titled “Checks before and during a run”](#checks-before-and-during-a-run) `.run` goes through these stages in order: build and validate the input (invalid input returns its failure right there), instantiate, check for missing methods, check verb positions on the first run, then run the steps (AC-B#runorder). * **Missing methods.** Each step, hook and case-branch method has to exist on the instance, public or private, and can’t come from `Kernel`, `Object` or `BasicObject`, so `step :puts` fails. The error is a `DefinitionError`: `<Action> declares step(s) calling :a, :b but does not implement them`. A step whose guard would skip it is still checked. * **Verb position.** The first run of a class raises `DefinitionError` when a plain verb has more code after it. [Errors and guardrails](/reference/actionable/errors/) has the details. ## See also [Section titled “See also”](#see-also) * [Control flow](/reference/actionable/control-flow/): the verbs a step calls * [Hooks and history](/reference/actionable/hooks-and-history/): what runs after the steps * [Declared outcomes](/reference/actionable/declared-outcomes/): codes a nested child may fail with

# Batches and introspection

> Run an action over many items with run_each, look actions up in the registry, and describe an action as data or text.

Three tools here treat actions as data. `run_each` feeds many inputs to one action, `Actionable.registry` finds an action by name, and `describe` reads an action’s shape without running it. ## `run_each` [Section titled “run_each”](#run_each) 
```ruby
Action.run_each(items) { |item| args } # => BatchResult
```
 The action runs once per item, in order, and each run is independent of the others. You get back a `BatchResult` that keeps the same order. The block turns an item into run arguments: a Hash becomes keywords, an Array becomes positional arguments, and anything else is one positional argument. With no block, items follow that same rule. One item failing never affects another, and nothing wraps the whole batch in a transaction (AC-B#runeach). 
```ruby
class TrimLoad < Actionable::Action
  input { required :cars, :integer }
  output { required :axles, :integer }
  fails :uneven


  step :weigh


  private


  def weigh
    fail!(:uneven) if input.cars.odd?
    @axles = input.cars * 3
  end
end


batch = TrimLoad.run_each([5, 6, 7, 8]) { |cars| { cars: cars } }
batch.size                     # => 4
batch.all_ok?                  # => false
batch.any_failure?             # => true
batch.partial?                 # => true
batch.successes.map(&:axles)   # => [18, 24]
batch.failures.size            # => 2
batch.skips.size              # => 0
batch[0].code                  # => :uneven
batch[7]                       # => nil
batch.map(&:status)            # => [:failure, :success, :failure, :success]
batch.to_api_h(id_field: :axles).first # => {index: 0, status: :failure, id: nil, errors: {}}
```
 ### `BatchResult` [Section titled “BatchResult”](#batchresult) `BatchResult` includes Enumerable over its results, and `each` with no block gives you an enumerator. | Method | Returns | | -------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | `[]` | the result at an index, `nil` out of range | | `size`, `length`, `results` | the count, and the results | | `empty?` | true with no results | | `all_ok?` | true when every result is `ok?` (vacuously true when empty) | | `any_failure?` | true when any result is a failure | | `partial?` | `any_failure?` and at least one ok result; false when empty or all failed | | `successes`, `failures`, `skips` | results selected by outcome | | `to_api_h(id_field: :id)` | `result.to_api_h(index: i, id_field:)` for each result, in order | ## The registry [Section titled “The registry”](#the-registry) `Actionable.registry` is one `Registry` per process. A named subclass lands in it the moment it is created. `Registry#register` overwrites whatever class already holds the name, skips anonymous classes, and returns the registry. Registration happens at subclass creation, so a `Class.new(Action)` you name later never gets in. Entries are never removed, and the registry takes no lock. | Method | Returns | | ---------------------------------- | -------------------------------------------------------- | | `[]` | the class for a String or Symbol name, `nil` if unknown | | `keys`, `values`, `size`, `empty?` | the usual | | `each` | yields `(name, class)`, or an enumerator without a block | | `register(klass)` | the registry | 
```ruby
class Pilotage < Actionable::Action; end


registry = Actionable.registry
registry[:Pilotage]               # => Pilotage
registry['Pilotage']              # => Pilotage
registry['Unmoored']              # => nil
registry.keys.include?('Pilotage') # => true
registry.values.include?(Pilotage) # => true
registry.empty?                   # => false
registry.register(Class.new(Actionable::Action)).equal?(registry) # => true
registry.each.first.class         # => Array
```
 ## `describe` [Section titled “describe”](#describe) 
```ruby
Action.describe # => Hash
```
 The Hash `describe` returns has exactly nine keys. | Key | Value | | ------------------- | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | `name` | the class name, or `nil` when anonymous | | `input` | the input schema’s `metadata.to_h`; `{discriminated_on:, shapes:}` for a dispatched action; `nil` when free-form | | `output` | the output schema’s `metadata.to_h`, or `nil` | | `steps` | `[{type:, name:, options:}]`; `type` is `:method`, `:action` or `:case`; an action step’s `name` is the class | | `failures`, `skips` | `{code => {message:, metadata:}}`, plus `output:` when the code has a schema | | `hooks` | `{on_success:, on_failure:, on_skip:, always:}` as name lists | | `measure` | the mode Symbol | | `transactional` | the transaction config, or `nil` | On a dispatched action, `shapes` lists the values each branch matches on, not the schema classes the name might suggest. Load the Rails adapter and `hooks` also carries an `after_commit` key, there even when empty. 
```ruby
class Sounding < Actionable::Action
  input { required :berth, :string }
  fails :aground, 'keel touched bottom', retry: true
  skips :slack
  step :lower_line
  always :coil_line


  private


  def lower_line = nil
  def coil_line = nil
end


description = Sounding.describe
description.keys.size                # => 9
description[:name]                   # => "Sounding"
description[:steps]                  # => [{type: :method, name: :lower_line, options: {}}]
description[:hooks][:always]         # => [:coil_line]
description[:failures]               # => {aground: {message: "keel touched bottom", metadata: {retry: true}}}
description[:input].keys             # => [:berth]
description[:output]                 # => nil
description[:measure]                # => :none
description[:transactional]          # => nil
```
 ## `describe_text` [Section titled “describe_text”](#describe_text) `describe_text` prints those same facts as a multi-line String, with no trailing newline: 1. `<name or "action"> (measure: <mode>)`, with `, transactional` after the mode when a transaction config exists. 2. `Input:` followed by `(free-form)`, or `(discriminated on <name>: <variants>)`, or the schema’s field lines indented two extra spaces. 3. `Output:` the same way. 4. `Steps:` and one `- <name> (<kind>)` line per step, or `(none)`. 5. `Failures:` and `Skips:`, only when non-empty, one `- <code>` line each, with the metadata in parentheses and `: <message>` when there is one. 6. `Hooks:`, only when some list is non-empty, one `<section>: <names>` line per non-empty section. The field lines are FieldStruct’s own description, so they change with its version. 
```ruby
lines = Sounding.describe_text.lines.map(&:chomp)
lines.first                    # => "Sounding (measure: none)"
lines.include?('  Steps:')     # => true
lines.include?('    - lower_line (method)') # => true
lines.include?('    - aground (retry: true): keel touched bottom') # => true
lines.last                     # => "    always: coil_line"
```
 ## See also [Section titled “See also”](#see-also) * [Results](/reference/actionable/results/): `to_api_h` and the result shape * [Declared outcomes](/reference/actionable/declared-outcomes/): what `failures` and `skips` list * [Introspection and tooling](/reference/field-struct/introspection-and-tooling/): `metadata.to_h` for the schemas

# Control flow

> The verbs a step uses to end a run, how halting works, the rules for codes, and where a plain verb may go.

Steps close a run by recording an outcome through a verb. A plain verb records and lets the run continue, and a bang verb records and stops the main steps. Stopping is done with a `throw` and never with an exception (AC-I1), while a real exception from a step goes straight through `.run` (AC-I2). The verbs are all private, and `result` and `input` are the public readers. ## The verbs [Section titled “The verbs”](#the-verbs) | Verb | Records | Returns | | -------------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------ | ------------- | | `succeed(message = nil, **output)` | `Success` | `true` | | `fail(code, message = nil, **errors)` | `Failure` | `false` | | `skip(code = :skipped, message = nil, **output)` | `Skipped` | `false` | | `fail_with(source, code: :invalid, message: nil, output: nil)` | `Failure` with `source.errors` | `false` | | `succeed!`, `fail!`, `skip!`, `fail_with!` | the same, then `halt!` | never returns | | `halt!` | nothing | never returns | Whichever record comes last wins (AC-I4). If nothing was recorded, the run succeeds by itself with code `:success`. Hooks still fire after a halt (see [Hooks and history](/reference/actionable/hooks-and-history/)). 
```ruby
class Refloat < Actionable::Action
  step :strand
  step :tow


  private


  def strand = fail(:adrift)
  def tow = succeed('refloated')
end


Refloat.run.success? # => true
Refloat.run.message  # => "refloated"
```
 ## `succeed` and `skip` [Section titled “succeed and skip”](#succeed-and-skip) Whatever keywords follow the message are output. With an `output` schema they survive only when they match its fields, and they override instance variables of the same name (see [Input and output](/reference/actionable/input-and-output/)). With no output schema, the keywords turn into the output Hash untouched. 
```ruby
class LogCrossing < Actionable::Action
  step :record


  private


  def record = succeed('logged', passengers: 41)
end


LogCrossing.run.output # => {passengers: 41}
```
 `skip` accepts a code and defaults to `:skipped`. It isn’t a failure, and `ok?` is true for it (AC-I3). 
```ruby
class RefuelTender < Actionable::Action
  step :refuel


  private


  def refuel = skip!(:tank_full, 'no fuel needed')
end


RefuelTender.run.skipped? # => true
RefuelTender.run.code     # => :tank_full
RefuelTender.run.ok?      # => true
```
 ## `fail` [Section titled “fail”](#fail) Each keyword after the message is an error, written `field => message` or `field => [messages]`. No keyword is held back for metadata, so `fail!(:x, nil, kind: 'is not valid')` produces `errors[:kind]`. Values become Strings, element by element for an Array. 
```ruby
class DenyBoarding < Actionable::Action
  step :check


  private


  def check = fail!(:denied, 'cannot board', pass: ['is expired', 'is unsigned'])
end


result = DenyBoarding.run
result.errors.to_h # => {pass: ["is expired", "is unsigned"]}
result.message     # => "cannot board"
```
 In an action, `fail` records a failure and is not `Kernel#fail`. To raise an exception, call `raise`. ### Codes [Section titled “Codes”](#codes) Codes have to be identifiers, matching `/\A[[:alpha:]_][[:alnum:]_]*[?!]?\z/`. Passing a sentence raises `ArgumentError` at the call. `fail 'berth unavailable'` is a `Kernel#fail` habit, and storing it as a code would bury the mistake. A String code is converted to a Symbol (AC §5.2). 
```ruby
class Phrase < Actionable::Action
  step :check


  private


  def check = fail('berth unavailable')
end


begin
  Phrase.run
rescue ArgumentError => e
  e.class # => ArgumentError
end
```
 After an action declares failure codes or skip codes, it can only end with declared ones. [Declared outcomes](/reference/actionable/declared-outcomes/) has the rules. ## `fail_with` [Section titled “fail_with”](#fail_with) `fail_with(source, code: :invalid, message: nil, output: nil)` takes `source.errors` from any object whose errors answer `to_h`, usually a FieldStruct, and so turns the validation of a side struct into a failure. The default code is `:invalid`, so an action that declares failures must declare that one or pass a different code. 
```ruby
class Quay < FieldStruct::Base
  required :harbour, :string
end


class Disembark < Actionable::Action
  step :check


  private


  def check = fail_with!(Quay.new, code: :bad_quay)
end


Disembark.run.errors.to_h # => {harbour: ["is required"]}
Disembark.run.code        # => :bad_quay
```
 `output:` counts as output and not as an error key only if the failure code declares an output schema (or the action uses typed outcomes). In any other case `fail_with` rejects it. See [Declared outcomes](/reference/actionable/declared-outcomes/). ## `halt!` and the throw [Section titled “halt! and the throw”](#halt-and-the-throw) `halt!` records nothing, stops the main steps and keeps whatever result exists. The tag it throws is `Actionable::HALT` (`:actionable_halt`), and the runner wraps steps in `Actionable.catch_halt`. 
```ruby
class DropAnchor < Actionable::Action
  step :near_shore
  step :open_sea


  private


  def near_shore
    succeed('anchored')
    halt!
  end


  def open_sea = succeed('underway')
end


DropAnchor.run.message # => "anchored"
Actionable::HALT       # => :actionable_halt
```
 ## Where a plain verb may go [Section titled “Where a plain verb may go”](#where-a-plain-verb-may-go) When a plain verb has more code after it in the same method, that code still runs, and nearly always a bang was forgotten. A class’s first `.run` reads its step and hook methods with Prism and raises `Actionable::DefinitionError` that names each offense, once per class (AC §5.14). The RuboCop cop `Actionable/VerbNotLast` flags the same things in your editor (see [RuboCop cop](/reference/actionable/rubocop/)). A plain verb is fine when it is: * the last statement of the method (a modifier `fail :x if bad?` counts as the statement) * the last statement of a branch (`if`, `unless`, ternary, `case`, `rescue`, `ensure`) whose construct is itself last * the right side of `&&`, `and`, `||` or `or` in last position * returned: `return fail(:x) if bad?`, or followed directly by `return` * the last statement of a block or loop body whose owning call is last * followed by an `ensure` clause * called on another receiver (`timetable.fail`, `legs.skip(1)`), which is never checked These are offenses: the verb has more code after it, it sits in a branch whose construct is followed by more code, it is the left side of `and` or `or` (`fail(:x) and return` never reaches `return`, because `fail` returns false), it is nested inside a bigger expression (`log(fail(:x))`, `@ok = fail(:x)`), or it appears in a `begin` body that has an `else`. Bang verbs are never checked, and neither are helper methods that aren’t declared steps or hooks. 
```ruby
def depart
  fail(:fog_bound) if harbour.fogged?   # offense: cast_off still runs
  cast_off
end


def depart
  fail!(:fog_bound) if harbour.fogged?  # good
  cast_off
end
```
 Because the check parses the method’s source file, it skips methods that have none readable (`eval`, `define_method`) and files that don’t parse. A subclass gets checked against its own set of methods. Passing is remembered, while failing raises again on every run until it is fixed. To switch it off for the process: 
```ruby
Actionable.verb_position_check?        # => true
Actionable.verb_position_check = false
Actionable.verb_position_check?        # => false
Actionable.verb_position_check = true
Actionable::VerbPosition.prism?        # => true
```
 ## See also [Section titled “See also”](#see-also) * [Results](/reference/actionable/results/): what each verb produces * [Declared outcomes](/reference/actionable/declared-outcomes/): restricting the codes * [Errors and guardrails](/reference/actionable/errors/): the checks that raise

# Declared failures, skips and typed outcomes

> Announce the codes an action can end with, attach messages and metadata, and give each code its own output.

With `fails` and `skips` an action lists the codes it can end with. That list is a contract: as soon as one code is declared, only declared codes are allowed (AC-I7). The core stores and shows the metadata but never interprets it, and the job adapter is the single reader, and only after an explicit opt-in. Typed outcomes take it a step further by giving each code its own output and result class. ## `fails` and `skips` [Section titled “fails and skips”](#fails-and-skips) 
```ruby
fails(code, message = nil, **metadata, &output_schema)
skips(code, message = nil, **metadata, &output_schema)
```
 Both keep `{message:, metadata:}` under the code (a Symbol or String, turned into a Symbol), and the metadata is frozen. Any failure or skip ending with that code exposes the metadata as `result.metadata`, plus the declared message when the call passed none, since an explicit message always wins. Subclasses get a copy of the declarations and may add to them or redeclare. A redeclaration replaces message, metadata and schema together, so leaving out the block drops the parent’s schema. 
```ruby
class HoldBerth < Actionable::Action
  fails :deck_full, 'no berth free on this deck', retry: false
  fails :harbour_closed, retry: true
  skips :already_held


  step :hold


  private


  def hold = fail!(:deck_full)
end


result = HoldBerth.run
result.message  # => "no berth free on this deck"
result.metadata # => {retry: false}
HoldBerth.declared_failures # => {deck_full: {retry: false}, harbour_closed: {retry: true}}
HoldBerth.declared_skips    # => {already_held: {}}
HoldBerth.failure_declarations[:deck_full] # => {message: "no berth free on this deck", metadata: {retry: false}}
HoldBerth.skip_declarations.keys           # => [:already_held]
```
 | Reader | Returns | | --------------------------------------------------------- | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | `declared_failures`, `declared_skips` | `{code => metadata}` | | `failure_declarations`, `skip_declarations` | `{code => {message:, metadata:}}`, plus `:output_schema` when the declaration had a block | | `failure_output_schema(code)`, `skip_output_schema(code)` | the code’s schema class, or `nil` | | `allows_failure_code?(code)` | true when no failures are declared, the code is declared, or it is a gem code | | `allows_skip_code?(code)` | true when no skips are declared or the code is declared | ## Strictness [Section titled “Strictness”](#strictness) Declare one failure code and the action may only fail with declared codes, plus the gem’s own `:invalid_input` and `:invalid_output`. Every other `fail`, `fail!`, `fail_with` or `fail_with!` raises `DefinitionError` at the call, and that includes the default `:invalid` of `fail_with`. Skips obey the same rule with no exceptions, since the gem emits no skip codes of its own: once a skip is declared, even the implicit `:skipped` of a bare `skip!` needs a declaration. Declaring failures doesn’t make skips strict, nor the reverse, and an action declaring neither is unchecked. A failure adopted from a nested child gets the same check (AC §5.9). 
```ruby
class CastOff < Actionable::Action
  fails :moored


  step :untie


  private


  def untie = fail!(:other)
end


begin
  CastOff.run
rescue Actionable::DefinitionError => e
  e.message # => "CastOff fails with :other, which it does not declare; declare it with `fails :other` (declared: :moored)"
end


CastOff.allows_failure_code?('moored')         # => true
CastOff.allows_failure_code?(:invalid_input)   # => true
CastOff.allows_failure_code?(:other)           # => false
CastOff.allows_skip_code?(:whatever)           # => true
```
 `fail` checks in this order: first that the code is an identifier, then that it is declared, and with typed outcomes the output checks come last. ### Gem codes [Section titled “Gem codes”](#gem-codes) `:invalid_input` and `:invalid_output` are allowed at all times. Declaring either one only puts metadata on the failures the gem builds, and when they occur stays the same. Neither takes an output schema, so a block on them raises `ArgumentError` at declaration. 
```ruby
class ReadSignal < Actionable::Action
  input { required :flags, :string }
  fails :invalid_input, 'garbled signal', retry: false


  step :read


  private


  def read = succeed
end


ReadSignal.run.metadata # => {retry: false}
ReadSignal.run.message  # => "input failed validation"
```
 ## Per-code output schemas [Section titled “Per-code output schemas”](#per-code-output-schemas) Give `fails` or `skips` a block and it becomes that code’s output schema, using the same DSL and the same reserved-name check as `output`. A result carrying such a code is assembled from the keywords it received and never from instance variables, then coerced and validated as a success is. If the output breaks the schema, the run ends as `Failure(:invalid_output)`. The block is an opt-in of its own, so all this holds with or without typed outcomes. For a failure code that has a schema, `fail`, `fail!`, `fail_with` and `fail_with!` receive the output through the `output:` keyword, as a Hash or a FieldStruct, and never as an error key. A skip receives it as keywords, like `succeed`. 
```ruby
class BookCabin < Actionable::Action
  input { required :bunks, :integer }
  fails(:deck_full) { required :free_bunks, :integer }


  step :allocate


  private


  def allocate = fail!(:deck_full, 'not enough bunks', output: { free_bunks: 2 })
end


result = BookCabin.run(bunks: 5)
result.code              # => :deck_full
result.output.free_bunks # => 2
BookCabin.failure_output_schema(:deck_full).attribute_names # => [:free_bunks]
```
 ## Typed outcomes [Section titled “Typed outcomes”](#typed-outcomes) 
```ruby
typed_outcomes                  # turn on
typed_outcomes(enabled: false)  # turn off
typed_outcomes?                 # read
```
 Turn typed outcomes on and `.run` hands back the action’s own `Success`, `Failure` and `Skipped` classes. They live as constants inside the action and inherit from the gem’s classes, so `is_a?(Actionable::Failure)` keeps working. When the parent action has typed outcomes, they inherit from the parent’s classes instead. The flag is copied as a snapshot, and a subclass can switch it off for itself. If the action already defines a constant named `Success`, `Failure` or `Skipped`, enabling the flag raises `DefinitionError`. Each code has a contract of its own. A failure or skip whose code has no schema carries `{}` as output, no matter what the steps left behind, and passing non-empty output raises `DefinitionError` at the call. An empty Hash is fine, and an `output:` that is neither a Hash nor a FieldStruct raises `ArgumentError`. The gem’s own failures are exempt: `invalid_output` carries the invalid struct and `invalid_input` always has `{}`. 
```ruby
class ChartRoute < Actionable::Action
  typed_outcomes


  input { required :leagues, :integer }
  output { required :hours, :integer }


  fails(:too_far) { required :range_leagues, :integer }


  step :plan


  private


  def plan
    fail!(:too_far, output: { range_leagues: 40 }) if input.leagues > 40
    @hours = input.leagues * 2
  end
end


ok = ChartRoute.run(leagues: 12)
ok.class     # => ChartRoute::Success
ok.hours     # => 24


failure = ChartRoute.run(leagues: 90)
failure.class                          # => ChartRoute::Failure
failure.range_leagues                  # => 40
failure.is_a?(Actionable::Failure)     # => true
ChartRoute.typed_outcomes?             # => true
```
 Equality goes by class, so with typed outcomes `ChartRoute.run(leagues: 12) == Actionable::Success.new(...)` is false, and you compare against the action’s own class instead. When a child’s failure crosses into a typed parent, it turns into the parent’s own `Failure`, judged by the parent’s schema for that code. The parent still has to declare the code, and output that breaks the parent’s schema ends the parent as `invalid_output`. A parent without typed outcomes receives a plain `Actionable::Failure`. The gem’s two own codes cross with their output as it was (AC §5.10). ## See also [Section titled “See also”](#see-also) * [Control flow](/reference/actionable/control-flow/): the verbs these codes are used with * [Background jobs](/reference/actionable/jobs/): `honor_retry_metadata` reads the metadata * [Testing](/reference/actionable/testing/): stubs check codes against declarations * [RBS](/reference/actionable/rbs/): the types typed outcomes generate

# Errors and guardrails

> Every exception class the gem raises and each check that raises it, with the message you will see.

There are only a few exception classes, but a lot of checks happen at declaration time, so a misconfigured action fails loudly the first time it loads or runs. Each message names the action (`action` when the class is anonymous) and what is wrong. One rule is different from the rest: a real exception raised inside a step, hook or guard passes straight out of `.run`, because the runner never rescues one (AC-I2). ## Exception classes [Section titled “Exception classes”](#exception-classes) | Class | Raised when | | ---------------------------------------- | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | `Actionable::Error` | base of everything this gem raises | | `Actionable::DefinitionError` | a misconfigured action (below); also used by the adapters | | `Actionable::Job::RetryableFailure` | `run_actionable` for a result that should be retried; has `result` | | `Actionable::Coverage::BaselineMismatch` | the coverage report differs from its baseline | | `ArgumentError` | a bad argument: a code that is not an identifier, an unsupported step target, a bad `measure` mode | | `FieldStruct::UnknownAttributeError` | input with `unknown_attributes :raise` and an extra keyword | `DefinitionError`, `RetryableFailure` and `BaselineMismatch` all inherit from `Actionable::Error`. The coverage report also raises `Actionable::Error` itself, for a missing baseline file or for a glob that matches no shard files. 
```ruby
Actionable::DefinitionError.ancestors.include?(Actionable::Error) # => true
Actionable::Error.superclass # => StandardError
FieldStruct::UnknownAttributeError.ancestors.include?(Actionable::Error) # => false
```
